Gravitational waves from quasielliptic compact binaries in scalar-tensor theory to one-and-a-half post-Newtonian order
David Trestini

Abstract
The orbit-averaged fluxes of energy and angular momentum generated by a compact binary system of nonspinning particles are obtained in a popular class of massless scalar-tensor theories with first-and-a-half post-Newtonian (1.5PN) accuracy, i.e., 2.5PN accuracy beyond the leading − 1 -1 PN dipolar radiation. The secular evolution of the orbital elements (frequency and eccentricity) are then also obtained with 1.5PN accuracy. Three technical advances were necessary to obtain these results: (i) the decomposition of the scalar dipole moment as a Fourier series at 1PN order, along with the other moments at Newtonian order, (ii) the derivation of the formula for the passage to the center-of-mass frame at 2.5PN, including a novel hereditary term arising from the contribution of the radiation, and (iii) the complete treatment of the memory-like term appearing in the angular momentum flux. Moreover, as a byproduct of this work, I revisit in the Appendix the treatment in general relativity of the memory integral appearing at 2.5PN order in the angular momentum flux [Phys. Rev. D 80 , 124018 (2009)] , and find that the spurious divergence in the initial eccentricity e 1 e_{1} found there is cured by a careful split between AC and DC terms.



pacs
04.25.Nx, 04.30.-w, 97.60.Jd, 97.60.Lf

I
Introduction

General relativity (GR) still passes with flying colors all precision astrophysical tests so far, such as solar system tests
Bertotti et al. 2003
;
Fomalont and Kopeikin 2003
;
Kopeikin and Fomalont 2007
;
Fomalont et al. 2010
;
Williams et al. 2004
, binary pulsar tests
Damour and Esposito-Farèse 1992
;
Fujii and Maeda 2005
;
De Felice and Tsujikawa 2010
;
Kramer et al. 2006
;
Kramer et al. 2021
and gravitational wave constraints
Abbott et al. 2021
. However, it is expected that GR is only the low-energy limit of some UV complete theory of quantum gravity, and deviations to GR would first manifest themselves as some extra degrees of freedom, such as a scalar field. This could be useful in explaining phenomena such as the accelerating universe, inflation, the Hubble tension
Smith et al. 2020
, etc.

Our understanding of GR itself has also dramatically improved. Focusing on gravitational waveforms, we now have post-Newtonian waveform templates at very high 4.5PN order
Blanchet, Luc and Faye, Guillaume and Henry, Quentin
and Larrouturou, François and Trestini,
David 2023a
;
Blanchet, Luc and Faye, Guillaume and Henry, Quentin
and Larrouturou, François and Trestini,
David 2023b
, numerical relativity templates are now full blown
Ajith et al. 2012
;
Ferguson et al. 2023
;
Boyle et al. 2019
, gravitational self-force techniques have yielded generic waveform template at adiabatic order
Hughes et al. 2021
;
Isoyama et al. 2022
and waveforms for circular orbits at second post-adiabatic order
Wardell et al. 2023
, and methods such as effective-one-body (EOB)
Buonanno and Damour 1999
;
Ramos-Buades et al. 2023
;
Gamba et al. 2023
or phenomenological models
Pratten et al. 2020
;
Williams et al. 2024
have been very successful at unifying these different waveforms.

In parallel, there has been a recent effort towards developing a complete template bank in alternative theories of gravity. To date, the most studied alternative theory is a class of scalar-tensor theories introduced by Damour and Esposito-Farèse
Damour and Esposito-Farèse 1996
(see Ref.
Mirshekari and Will 2013
for an equivalent formulation, which I will be using), which generalizes Brans-Dicke gravity
Brans and Dicke 1961
;
Fierz 1956
. This theory has first been studied within the post-Newtonian framework, focusing on the equations of motion
Mirshekari and Will 2013
;
Bernard 2018
;
Bernard 2019
and waveform templates for nonspinning circular orbits
Lang 2015
;
Lang 2014
;
Sennett and Buonanno 2016
;
Bernard et al. 2022
. There has also been recent interest in studying the post-Newtonian dynamics within the EFT framework
Almeida 2024
;
Diedrichs et al. 2023
, in which the spinning case
Almeida and Zhou 2024
and tidal effects
Bernard et al. 2023
were recently studied. These PN results have also been used to inform effective-one-body models
Julié and Deruelle 2017
;
Julié 2018
;
Julié et al. 2022
;
Jain et al. 2023
;
Jain 2023
.
Finallly, this class of theories has also recently been studied in the context of numerical relativity
Ma et al. 2023
;
Corman and East 2024
, which found good agreement with post-Newtonian templates in the region where they overlap.

The present work aims at generalizing some of the results obtained for nonspinning
quasicircular
orbits in Ref.
Bernard et al. 2022
, henceforth referred to as
35
, to the case of nonspinning
quasielliptic
orbits. It crucially relies on the recent quasi-Keplerian parametrization at 2PN order for ST theories obtained in Ref.
Trestini 2024
, henceforth referred to as
47
. The main result I will be deriving is the orbit-averaged fluxes of energy and angular momentum at 1.5PN (i.e. 2.5PN beyond the leading
-1
PN dipolar radiation), along with the secular evolution of the orbital parameters (e.g. the frequency and eccentricity) at 2.5PN. The latter result generalizes the “chirp” to the case of quasielliptic orbits, and is thus the main GW observable. To this end, two technical advances were needed. First, the expansion of scalar dipole moment as a Fourier series in time is computed for the first time with 1PN accuracy, along with the other required moments at Newtonian order. Secondly, the formula for the passage to the center-of-mass (CM) frame is obtained for the first time at 2.5PN order, including a novel hereditary term that arises from the contribution of the radiation to the definition of the CM frame, through the fluxes of linear momentum and CM position. This extra contribution was discovered only very recently in the context of GR
Blanchet et al. 2024
. Thirdly, the tail sector of the flux is computed for the first time in terms of enhancement functions, whose small-eccentricity expansions are also presented. Finally, the memory piece of the angular momentum flux is treated consistently, where the AC part is computed using the Fourier expansion of the scalar dipole moment, while the DC part is treated thanks to the secular evolution equations of the orbital parameters derived in Ref.
Trestini 2024
.

Despite the stringent constraints on this class of theories, stemming mostly from the constraints on the existence of dipolar radiation
Barausse et al. 2016
, the motivation for this work is two-fold. First, future ground-based detectors such as Einstein Telescope
Maggiore et al. 2020
or Cosmic Explorer
Abbott et al. 2017
will have drastically higher sensitivities than current ones, and will be able to place much more stringent constraints on deviations from GR. In this context, it is interesting to have alternative template banks for popular theories that cover the entire parameter space (masses, spins, eccentricity, tidal effects, …), e.g., in the event that we detect an alternative signal with very small coupling constants. Moreover, it is not excluded that a subpopulation of signals exhibit GW signals that differ greatly from GR, and have thus been missed by matched filtering
Magee et al. 2023
— alternative templates would be very useful in this regard. The second motivation is that this class of theories is simple enough to explore the phenomenology of extra degrees of freedom at high post-Newtonian order, and serve as a testbed for more complicated theories. For example, there has been recent interest for scalar-Gauss-Bonnet theories, which build on results in ST theories to obtain their waveforms
Shiralilou et al. 2022
;
Julié and Berti 2019
;
Julié et al. 2022
;
Julié et al. 2024
;
Corman and East 2024
. Moreover, more general theories, e.g. in the Horndeski class, can exhibit screening mechanisms which cannot yet be correctly treating within the post-Newtonian framework
Bošković and Barausse 2023
;
de Rham et al. 2024
. Thus, I view this program as an incremental step towards the ultimate goal of obtaining waveforms for some robust future extension of general relativity.

The plan of the paper is as follows. After notational reminders, I will introduce in Sec.
II
the ST theory under scrutiny, and review the quasi-Keplerian parametrization at 2PN for ST theories in Sec.
III
. In Sec.
IV
, I will go over the crucial step of decomposing the multipole moments into Fourier series. In Sec.
V
, I will discuss the passage to the CM frame, with the appearance of non-local terms first discovered in Ref.
Blanchet et al. 2024
. The fluxes of energy and angular momentum for eccentric orbits will be derived in Sec.
VI
at 1.5PN order beyond quadrupolar radiation (i.e. 2.5PN beyond the leading
-1
PN dipolar radiation), and will be used in Sec
VII
to obtain the secular evolution of the orbital elements through 2.5PN order. After a short conclusion in Sec.
VIII
, many lengthy coefficients are relegated in the Appendices. Most results are also given in machine-readable form in the Supplemental Material
Sup
. Finally, I complete
in general relativity
the memory piece of the angular momentum flux in App.
A
, curing the spurious divergence in the initial eccentricity appearing in the previous treatment of Ref.
Arun et al. 2009
.

Main notations and summary of parameters

The convention in this work is that all stated PN orders are, by default, relative to the the Newtonian dynamics and to the standard quadrupole radiation in GR. Thus, the dominant dipole radiation enters at
-0.5
PN order in the waveform and at
-1
PN order in the energy flux. The QK parametrization at second post-Newtonian order computed in
47
is therefore next-to-next-to-leading-order, while the Newtonian fluxes and waveforms are next-to-leading order.

After a 3+1 decomposition of spacetime, and using the convention that boldface letters represent three-dimensional Euclidean vectors, the field-point spatial vector in radiative coordinates is denoted by
\bm{X}=R\,\bm{N}
, where
\bm{N}
has unit norm. In spherical coordinates, the coordinates of the field point are denoted by
(R,\Theta,\Phi)
. Time is denoted by
t
, and retarded time is defined as
t_{\text{ret}}=t-R/c
, so as to not confuse it with the eccentric anomaly of the QK motion,
u
.

The positions of particles 1 and 2 are denoted by
\bm{y}_{1}
and
\bm{y}_{2}
, and
\bm{x}=\bm{y}_{1}-\bm{y}_{2}
is the separation vector. The orbital radius is given by
r=|\bm{x}|
, and the unit vector
\bm{n}=\bm{x}/r
is introduced. The relative velocity is given by
\bm{v}=\differential\bm{x}/\differential t
. One can then introduce the rotating orthonormal triad
(\bm{n},\bm{\lambda},\bm{\ell})
and the nonrotating reference triad
(\bm{n}_{0},\bm{\lambda}_{0},\bm{\ell}_{0})
, which are defined rigorously in Sec.
IV
. For nonspinning binaries, there is no precession, so
\ell=\ell_{0}
and one can describe the orbital motion with polar coordinates
(r,\phi)
in the orbital plane
1
1

1

The notation
\phi
is used both for the scalar field and the polar angle, but its meaning will always be clear in context. Moreover,
r
will be used at one occasion to denote the radial coordinate of the field point in harmonic coordinates, at this will be stated clearly.
such that
\bm{n}=\cos\phi\,\bm{n}_{0}+\sin\phi\,\bm{\lambda}_{0}
and
\bm{\lambda}=-\sin(\phi)\bm{n}_{0}+\cos(\phi)\bm{\lambda}_{0}
. Notice that the relative velocity is given by
\bm{v}=\dot{r}\bm{n}+r\dot{\phi}\bm{\lambda}
. The following relations then hold:

\bm{n}\cdot\bm{v}=\dot{r}
,
v^{2}=\dot{r}^{2}+r^{2}\dot{\phi}^{2}
, and
\bm{n}\times\bm{v}=r\,\dot{\phi}\,\bm{\ell}
.

The notation
L=i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}
stands for multi-index with
\ell
spatial indices (and
K
would stand for a multi-index with
k
spatial indices). One can then write
\partial_{L}=\partial_{i_{1}}\cdots\partial_{i_{\ell}}
,
\partial_{aL-1}=\partial_{a}\partial_{i_{1}}\cdots\partial_{i_{\ell-1}}
and so on; similarly,
n_{L}=n_{i_{1}}\cdots n_{i_{\ell}}
,
n_{aL-1}=n_{a}n_{i_{1}}\cdots n_{i_{\ell-1}}
. The symmetric trace-free (STF) part is indicated using a hat or angled brackets: for instance,
\mathrm{STF}_{L}[\partial_{L}]=\hat{\partial}_{L}=\partial_{\langle i_{1}}\partial_{i_{2}}\cdots\partial_{i_{\ell}\rangle}
,
\mathrm{STF}_{L}[n_{L}]=\hat{n}_{L}=n_{\langle i_{1}}n_{i_{2}}\cdots n_{i_{\ell}\rangle}
, and
\mathrm{STF}_{L}[x_{L}]=\hat{x}_{L}=r^{\ell}n_{\langle i_{1}}n_{i_{2}}\cdots n_{i_{\ell}\rangle}
.
The
n
th time derivative of a function
F(t)
is denoted
F^{(n)}(t)=\differential^{n}F/\differential t^{n}
.

The constant asymptotic value of the scalar field at spatial infinity is denoted
\phi_{0}
, and the normalized scalar field is defined by
\varphi\equiv\phi/\phi_{0}
. The Brans-Dicke-like scalar function
\omega(\phi)
is expanded around the asymptotic value
\omega_{0}\equiv\omega(\phi_{0})
and the mass functions
m_{A}(\phi)
(see Section
II
for a definition) are expanded around the asymptotic values
m_{A}\equiv m_{A}(\phi_{0})
, where
A\in\{1,2\}
. In the CM frame, one then defines the asymptotic total mass
m=m_{1}+m_{2}
, reduced mass
\mu=m_{1}m_{2}/m
, symmetric mass ratio
\nu=\mu/m\in\ ]0,1/4]
, and relative mass difference
\delta=(m_{1}-m_{2})/m\in\ ]1,1[
. Note that the asymptotic symmetric mass ratio and the relative mass difference are linked by the relation
\delta^{2}=1-4\nu
.

Following
Bernard 2018
;
Bernard et al. 2022
, a number of parameters describing these expansions are introduced in Table
1
. The ST parameters are defined directly from the expansions of the Brans-Dicke-like scalar function
\omega(\phi)
and of the mass functions
m_{A}(\phi)
. The PN parameters are combinations of the ST parameters that naturally extend and generalize the usual PPN parameters to the case of a general ST theory
Will and Nordtvedt Jr 1972
;
Will 2018
. Moreover, these parameters can be mapped back to those of the formulation of Damour and Esposito-Farèse
Damour and Esposito-Farèse 1996
, see e.g. Table I of Ref.
Jain and Rettegno 2024
for the complete correspondence.

Summary of parameters for the general ST theory and notations for PN parameters.

II
Massless scalar-tensor theories

As in
35
, I will consider a generic class of ST theories in which a single massless scalar field
\phi
minimally couples to the metric
g_{\mu\nu}
, described by the action:

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{ST}}

\displaystyle=\frac{c^{3}}{16\pi G}\int\differential^{4}x\,\sqrt{-g}\left[\phi R-\frac{\omega(\phi)}{\phi}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\phi\partial_{\beta}\phi\right]+S_{\mathrm{m}}\left(\mathfrak{m},g_{\alpha\beta}\right)\,,

(1)

where
R
and
g
are, respectively, the Ricci scalar and the determinant of the metric,
\omega
is a function of the scalar field and
\mathfrak{m}
stands generically for the matter fields. Since these theories break the strong equivalence principle, I will follow the standard prescription by Eardley
Eardley 1975
(see also
Nordtvedt 1990
), which consists in modeling the inertial masses of each particle as a function of the scalar field, namely
m_{A}(\phi)
, where
A\in\{1,2\}
for a binary system. The effective matter action for point particles thus reads

S_{\mathrm{m}}=-c\sum_{A}\int\,m_{A}(\phi)\sqrt{-\left(g_{\alpha\beta}\right)_{A}\differential y_{A}^{\alpha}\,\differential y_{A}^{\beta}}\,,

(2)

where
y_{A}^{\alpha}
denotes the space-time positions of the particles, and
\left(g_{\alpha\beta}\right)_{A}
is the metric evaluated
2
2

2

Divergences are treated with Hadamard regularization
Blanchet and Faye 2000
, which is equivalent, at this order, to dimensional regularization
Blanchet et al. 2004
;
Blanchet et al. 2005
.
at the position of particle
A
. Despite minimal coupling, the point particle effective model introduces a dependence in the scalar field.
The action of Eq. (
1
) is usually called the Jordan-frame action, as the matter only couples to the Jordan or “physical” metric
g_{\alpha\beta}
. It is then very useful to introduce a rescaled scalar field and a conformally related metric,

\varphi\equiv\frac{\phi}{\phi_{0}}\qquad\mathrm{and}\qquad\tilde{g}_{\alpha\beta}\equiv\varphi\,g_{\alpha\beta}\,,

(3)

where
\phi_{0}
is the constant cosmological background value of the scalar field, such that the physical and conformal metrics have the same asymptotic behavior at spatial infinity. Quantities expressed in terms of the pair
(\varphi,\tilde{g}_{\alpha\beta})
are said to be in the “Einstein frame.”
From these, one defines the scalar and metric perturbation variables
\psi\equiv\varphi-1
and
h^{\mu\nu}\equiv\sqrt{-\tilde{g}}\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}-\eta^{\mu\nu}
, where
\eta^{\mu\nu}~=~\text{diag}(-1,1,1,1)
is the Minkowski metric. Within this perturbative setup, the arbitrary Brans-Dicke coupling function will be Taylor-expanded around the cosmological background value
\phi_{0}
, namely
\omega(\phi)=\omega_{0}+(\phi-\phi_{0})\omega_{0}^{\prime}+\mathcal{O}\left((\phi-\phi_{0})^{2}\right)
. Moreover, the mass functions
m_{A}(\phi)
will be described perturbatively in terms of their sensitivites, defined as

s_{A}^{(k)}\equiv\evaluated{\frac{\dd^{k+1}\ln{m_A(\phi)}}{\dd(\ln{\phi})^{k+1}}}_{\phi=\phi_0}\,,

(4)

where
s_{A}\equiv s_{A}^{(1)}
.
In these theories, no hair theorems
Hawking 1972
;
Sotiriou and Faraoni 2012
impose that isolated black holes carry no scalar charge. This strongly suggests that black holes in a binary system also have vanishing scalar charge, and this is partially confirmed by numerical relativity computations
Healy et al. 2012
. Since the scalar charge
\alpha_{A}
of the particle
A
is related to its sensitivity by the relation
Sennett and Buonanno 2016

\alpha_{A}=\frac{1-2s_{A}}{\sqrt{3+2\omega_{0}}}\,,

(5)

this means that black holes in a binary system have sensitivity
s_{A}=1/2
. In all post-Newtonian computations, it was indeed found that the results of GR are recovered by setting
s_{1}=s_{2}=1/2
.

With all these tools in hand, the field equations in the Landau-Lifschitz formulation
Landau and Lifshitz 1971
can now be rewritten
Mirshekari and Will 2013

\displaystyle\Box_{\eta}\,h^{\mu\nu}=\frac{16\pi G}{c^{4}}\tau^{\mu\nu}\,,

(6a)

\displaystyle\Box_{\eta}\,\psi=-\frac{8\pi G}{c^{4}}\tau_{s}\,,

(6b)

where
\Box_{\eta}
denotes the ordinary flat space-time d’Alembertian operator, and where the source terms read

\displaystyle\tau^{\mu\nu}=\frac{\varphi}{\phi_{0}}(-g)T^{\mu\nu}+\frac{c^{4}}{16\pi G}\Lambda^{\mu\nu}[h,\psi]\,,

(7a)

\displaystyle\tau_{s}=-\frac{\varphi}{\phi_{0}(3+2\omega(\phi))}\sqrt{-g}\left(T-2\varphi\frac{\partial T}{\partial\varphi}\right)-\frac{c^{4}}{8\pi G}\Lambda_{s}[h,\psi]\,.

(7b)

Here
T^{\mu\nu}=2(-g)^{-1/2}\delta S_{\mathrm{m}}/\delta g_{\mu\nu}
is the matter stress-energy tensor,
T\equiv g_{\mu\nu}T^{\mu\nu}
is its trace and
\partial T/\partial\varphi
is defined as the partial derivative of
T(g_{\mu\nu},\varphi)
with respect to
\varphi
holding
g_{\mu\nu}
constant. Moreover,
\Lambda^{\mu\nu}
and
\Lambda^{s}
are given explicitly in (2.8) and (2.9) of
35
as functionals of the
h^{\mu\nu}
and
\psi
that are at least quadratic in the fields.

The field equations of (
6
) are solved using the PN-MPM construction
Blanchet and Damour 1986
;
Blanchet 2014
. The reader is invited to refer to
35
for a detailed description of this construction in the case of ST theories. The main point is that the fields in vacuum zone exterior to the source the are written as a formal expansion in the bookkeeping parameter
G
, namely
h^{\mu\nu}=Gh_{1}^{\mu\nu}+G^{2}h_{2}^{\mu\nu}+\mathcal{O}(G^{3})
and
\psi=G\psi_{1}+G^{2}\psi_{2}+\mathcal{O}(G^{3})
. The linearized piece is parametrized by the so-called source moments
\mathrm{I}_{L}
,
\mathrm{J}_{L}
and
\mathrm{I}_{L}^{s}
, along with some gauge moments
\mathrm{W}_{L}
,
\mathrm{X}_{L}
,
\mathrm{Y}_{L}
, and
\mathrm{Z}_{L}
, which are pure functions of retarded time and are symmetric trace-free (STF) in their indices. These moments uniquely parametrize the linearized metric
h_{1}^{\mu\nu}
and the linearized scalar field
\psi_{1}
. For example, the linearized scalar field reads explicitly

\psi_{1}=-\frac{2}{c^{2}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{+\infty}\frac{(-)^{\ell}}{\ell!}\,\partial_{L}\!\left[\frac{\mathrm{I}_{L}^{s}(t-r/c)}{r}\right]\,,

(8)

where in this particular case,
r
denotes the radial coordinate of the field point in harmonic coordinates (rather than the radial separation of the binary).
For a given source, these moments can be computed explicitly as functions of the orbital variables, see e.g. App. (B.1) of
35
. A feature of ST theory is that the scalar monopole
\mathrm{I}^{s}
is not constant but its time-variation will be a small PN effect, i.e.,
\differential\mathrm{I}^{s}/\differential t=\mathcal{O}(c^{-2})
. This is why the leading-order radiation is dipolar, and not monopolar. It is thus practical to define

\mathrm{I}^{s}=\frac{1}{\phi_{0}}\left[m^{s}+\frac{E^{s}}{c^{2}}\right]\,,

(9a)

where

m^{s}=-\frac{1}{3+2\omega_{0}}\sum_{A}m_{A}(1-2s_{A})

(9b)

is constant and
E^{s}
is the time-varying, PN correction.

The parametrized linearized piece is reinjected iteratively into the field equations (
6
), where
T^{\mu\nu}=0
, since only the vacuum zone is considered here. This yields field equations for
h_{n}^{\mu\nu}
and
\psi_{n}
(for
n\geq 2
) which can be iteratively solved at quadratic
Blanchet and Damour 1992
;
Blanchet 1998a
, cubic
Blanchet 1998b
;
Trestini et al. 2023
;
Trestini and Blanchet 2023
and quartic order
Marchand et al. 2016
. Once this MPM metric is constructed at desired order, one can extract its leading order expression in the large radii expansion (this is the only thing an asymptotic observer will see). This asymptotic metric, written in terms of some radiative coordinates
(t,R,\bm{N})
[or alternatively
(t,R,\Theta,\Phi)
] which describe the position of the field point, admits a multipolar decomposition as well, which is uniquely parametrized, after TT projection, by three families of
radiative
moments
Blanchet 2014
;
Bernard et al. 2022
, denoted
\mathcal{U}_{L}
,
\mathcal{V}_{L}
and
\mathcal{U}^{s}_{L}
. Introducing
t_{\text{ret}}=t-R/c
, namely retarded time in the radiative coordinate system
Blanchet 1987
;
Trestini et al. 2023
, one find that that the asymptotic metric reads in a transverse-traceless gauge

\displaystyle h_{ij}^{\text{TT}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4G}{c^{2}R}\perp^{\text{TT}}_{ijab}\sum_{\ell=2}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{c^{\ell}\ell!}\Bigg[N_{L-2}\,\mathcal{U}_{abL-2}(t_{\text{ret}})-\frac{2\ell}{c(\ell+1)}N_{cL-2}\epsilon_{cd(a}\mathcal{V}_{b)dL-2}(t_{\text{ret}})\Bigg]+\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{1}{R^{2}}\Big)\,,

(10a)

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle=-\frac{2G}{c^{2}R}\sum_{\ell=0}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{c^{\ell}\ell!}N_{L}\,\mathcal{U}_{L}^{s}(t_{\text{ret}})+\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{1}{R^{2}}\Big)\,,

(10b)

where
\perp_{ijab}^{\mathrm{TT}}=\perp_{a(i}\perp_{j)b}-\frac{1}{2}\!\perp_{ij}\perp_{ab}
with
\perp_{ij}=\delta_{ij}-N_{i}N_{j}
.
The radiative moments are generically related to the source moments by the relations

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{L}

\displaystyle=\overset{(\ell)}{\mathrm{I}}_{\!\!L}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{3}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\quad\mathcal{V}_{L}

\displaystyle=\overset{(\ell)}{\mathrm{J}}_{\!\!L}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{3}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\quad\mathcal{U}^{s}_{L}

\displaystyle=\overset{(\ell)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!\!}^{s}_{L}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{3}}\right)\,,

(11)

where the small
\mathcal{O}(1/c^{3})
corrections correspond to the nonlinear corrections of the MPM construction. The full expressions needed for the 1.5PN fluxes were obtained in
35
, and read

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}_{ij}}+\frac{2G\mathrm{M}}{\phi_{0}c^{3}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\differential\tau\,\overset{(4)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}_{ij}(t_{\text{ret}}-\tau)\left[\ln{\left( \frac{c\tau}{2b_0} \right)}+\frac{11}{12}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{G(3+2\omega_{0})}{3c^{3}}\Bigg(-\mathrm{I}^{s}_{\langle i}\overset{(3)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{j\rangle}-\overset{(1)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{\langle i}\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}^{s}_{j\rangle}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{I}^{s}\overset{(3)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{ij}+\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\differential\tau\,\Bigl[\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{\langle i}\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{j\rangle}\Bigr](t_{\text{ret}}-\tau)\Bigg)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{5}}\right)\,.

(12a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{s}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{I}^{s}+\frac{2G\mathrm{M}}{\phi_{0}^{2}c^{5}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,\overset{(2)}{E}{\!}^{s}(t_{\text{ret}}-\tau)\ln\left(\frac{c\tau}{2b_{0}}\right)+\frac{G}{c^{5}}\left(1-\frac{\phi_{0}\omega_{0}^{\prime}}{3+2\omega_{0}}\right)\Bigg[\frac{2}{9}\mathrm{I}_{k}^{s}\overset{(3)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}_{k}^{s}-2\,\mathrm{I}^{s}\frac{\overset{(1)}{E}{\!}^{s}}{\phi_{0}}\Bigg]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{7}}\right)\,,

(12b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{i}^{s}

\displaystyle=\overset{(1)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}_{i}^{s}+\frac{2G\mathrm{M}}{\phi_{0}c^{3}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,\overset{(3)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}_{i}^{s}(\tau)\left[\ln{\left(\frac{c\tau}{2b_0} \right)}+1\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\ +\frac{G}{c^{5}}\Bigg[-\frac{1}{5}\overset{(1)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{k}\overset{(3)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}_{ik}-\frac{1}{5}\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{k}\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}_{ik}+\frac{3}{5}\overset{(3)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{k}\overset{(1)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}_{ik}+\frac{3}{5}\overset{(4)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{k}\mathrm{I}_{ik}-\epsilon_{iab}\mathrm{J}_{a}\overset{(3)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{b}-4\overset{(1)}{\mathrm{W}}\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{i}-4\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{W}}\overset{(1)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{i}+4\mathrm{I}^{s}\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{Y}_{i}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\ +\Big(1-\frac{\phi_{0}\omega_{0}^{\prime}}{3+2\omega_{0}}\Big)\Bigg(-2\frac{\overset{(1)}{E}{\!}_{s}}{\phi_{0}}\overset{(1)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{i}-2\frac{\overset{(2)}{E}{\!}_{s}}{\phi_{0}}\mathrm{I}^{s}_{i}+\frac{2}{5}\overset{(3)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{k}\overset{(1)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{ik}+\frac{2}{5}\overset{(4)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{k}\mathrm{I}^{s}_{ik}\Bigg)\Bigg]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{7}}\right)\,,

(12c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{ij}^{s}

\displaystyle=\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}_{ij}^{s}+\frac{2G\mathrm{M}}{\phi_{0}c^{3}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,\overset{(4)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{ij}(t_{\text{ret}}-\tau)\left[\ln{\left(\frac{c\tau}{2b_0} \right)}+\frac{3}{2}\right]-\frac{G}{c^{3}}\mathrm{I}^{s}\overset{(3)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}_{ij}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{5}}\right)\,,

(12d)

where the instantaneous moments are implicitly evaluated at
t_{\text{ret}}
.
Note the presence of hereditary tails (associated as usual to the arbitrary length scale
b_{0}
) as well and memory integrals in the latter expressions.

III
The quasi-Keplerian parametrization

In previous works
Lang 2014
;
Lang 2015
;
Bernard et al. 2022
;
Jain and Rettegno 2024
, the fluxes of energy and angular momentum were computed in terms of the orbital variables of the binary in the CM frame, namely the relative position
\bm{x}=r\bm{n}
and the relative velocity
\bm{v}
. However, in order to provide a ready-to-use post-Newtonian waveform template, one still has to solve equations of motions such as to obtain a parametrization of the orbital motion, which is then explicitly injected into these fluxes. This parametrization is very easy if one assumes that the orbit is bound and quasicircular
Sennett et al. 2016
;
Bernard et al. 2022
, which is relevant astrophysically, since compact binaries are expected to have circularized by the time they enter the frequency band of ground based GW detectors. However, other mechanisms, such as von Zeipel–Lidov–Kozai resonances
Blaes et al. 2002
or dynamical captures, can lead to non-negligeable eccentricities even within the detector’s frequency band, and there are hints that eccentric binaries have in fact already been detected
Romero-Shaw et al. 2022
. For an quasielliptic orbit, one of the standard prescriptions in general relativity is the quasi-Keplerian (QK) parametrization
Damour and Deruelle 1985
, which have been fully extended up to 3PN order
Damour and Schäfer 1988
;
Schäfer and Wex 1993
;
Memmesheimer et al. 2004
, and partially up to 4PN order
Cho et al. 2022
(see also
Tucker and Will 2019
for an equivalent osculating-orbit prescription). In
47
, I have extended the QK parametrization to the case of scalar-tensor theories at 2PN order, which is exactly what will be needed for this work.
At 2PN order, the parametrization reads

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=a_{r}(1-e_{r}\cos(u))\,,

(13a)

\displaystyle\ell=n(t-t_{0})

\displaystyle=u-e_{t}\sin(u)+f_{t}\sin(v)+g_{t}(v-u)\,,

(13b)

\displaystyle\phi-\phi_{0}

\displaystyle=K\Big[v+f_{\phi}\sin(2v)+g_{\phi}\sin(3v)\Big]\,,

(13c)

where
t
is time,
(r,\phi)
are the polar coordinates in the orbital plane,
u
is an affine parameter called the
eccentric anomaly
, and the
true anomaly
is defined as

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=2\arctan[\sqrt{\frac{1+e_{\phi}}{1-e_{\phi}}}\tan\Big(\frac{u}{2}\Big)\Bigg]+2\pi\bigg\lfloor\frac{u+\pi}{2\pi}\bigg\rfloor

\displaystyle=u+2\arctan[\frac{\beta(e_{\phi})\sin(u)}{1-\beta(e_{\phi})\cos(u)}\Bigg]\,,

(14)

where

\beta(e)\equiv\frac{e}{1+\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}\,.

(15)

The quasi-Keplerian parameters are the mean motion
n
, the rate of pericenter advance
K
, the semi major axis
a_{r}
, the time eccentricity
e_{t}
, the radial eccentricity
e_{r}
, the angular eccentricity
e_{\phi}
, the rate of pericenter advance
K
, and the higher-order parameters
f_{\phi}
,
f_{t}
,
g_{\phi}
, and
g_{t}
. The motion is characterized by two generically incommensurable periods, namely the radial period
P
[i.e. the time between two pericenter passages], which is related to the mean motion by
n=2\pi/P
; and the angular period [i.e. the time needed for
\phi
to go from
0
to
2\pi
], related to the angular frequency
\omega=Kn
. The mean anomaly is then defined as
\ell=n(t-t_{0})
, and the advance in the pericenter is typically decomposed as
K=1+k
, where
k
is a small PN quantity. Moreover, the three eccentricities
e_{r}
,
e_{\phi}
and
e_{t}
differ by small PN corrections, and are thus equal in the Newtonian limit. It will also be extremely useful to introduce the small frequency-related PN parameter

x=\left(\frac{Gm\omega}{c^{3}}\right)^{2/3}=\left(\frac{GmnK}{c^{3}}\right)^{2/3}\,.

(16)

Crucially, these various parameters are explicitly expressed in terms of a set of two parameters that entirely characterize the orbits, e.g. the dimensionless energy and angular moment
(\varepsilon,j)
, which are defined from the mechanical energy and angular momentum
(E,J)
as

\varepsilon=-\frac{2E}{\mu c^{2}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad j=-\frac{2J^{2}E}{\mu^{3}(\tilde{G}\alpha m)^{2}}\,.

(17)

This is the main result of
Trestini 2024
, and the interested reader is encouraged to refer to it for more details. Another useful pair of gauge invariant variables is
(x,\iota)
where
\iota\equiv x\mathcal{B}/k
and

\mathcal{B}=3+2\bar{\gamma}-\bar{\beta}_{+}+\bar{\beta}_{-}\delta\,.

(18)

The variable
\iota
is defined
such that
\iota\rightarrow j
when
x\rightarrow 0
. However, the limiting case of circular orbit limit is not immediate to obtain with this choice. Thus, from now on, I will work with the pair of variables
(x,e_{t})
, which has the good property of yielding the results for circular orbits by taking the limit
e_{t}\rightarrow 0
, but has the unfortunate property of not being gauge invariant.

Moreover, for the purpose of the following derivations, it will be very practical to define

\displaystyle w=v(u)-u

\displaystyle=2\arctan\left[\frac{\beta(e_{\phi})\sin(u)}{1-\beta(e_{\phi})\cos(u)}\right]\,,

(19)

\displaystyle\tilde{w}

\displaystyle=2\arctan\left[\frac{\beta(e_{t})\sin(u)}{1-\beta(e_{t})\cos(u)}\right]\,,

(20)

where
\beta
is defined in Eq. (
15
).
We then denote
\tilde{v}=u+\tilde{w}
, such that
w
and
\tilde{w}
represent the periodically oscillating pieces of
v
and
\tilde{v}
.
Finally, I will often need to apply orbit averaging. For any time-depending quantity
f
, I define its orbit average over a radial period as

\left\langle f\right\rangle=\frac{1}{P}\int_{0}^{P}\differential t\,f(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\differential\ell\,f(\ell)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\differential u\,\frac{\differential\ell}{\differential u}\,f(u)\,,

(21)

where the 2PN-accurate expression for
\differential\ell/\differential u
can be derived from the results presented in
47
, and reads

\displaystyle\frac{\differential\ell}{\differential u}

\displaystyle=1-e_{t}\cos u+\frac{x^{2}}{8}\Bigg\{\frac{(1-e_{t}^{2})(15+8\bar{\gamma}-\nu)\nu}{\big[1-e_{t}\cos u\big]^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{1-e_{t}\cos u}\bigg[60+60\bar{\gamma}+15\bar{\gamma}^{2}-4\bar{\delta}_{+}-4\bar{\delta}_{-}\delta+\nu\,\big(\!-39-24\bar{\gamma}+8\bar{\beta}_{+}-8\bar{\beta}_{-}\delta\big)+\nu^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-e_{t}^{2}}}\bigg[60+60\bar{\gamma}+15\bar{\gamma}^{2}-4\bar{\delta}_{+}-4\bar{\delta}_{-}\delta+\nu\,\big(\!-24-16\bar{\gamma}+8\bar{\beta}_{+}-8\bar{\beta}_{-}\delta\big)\bigg]\Bigg\}+\mathcal{O}(x^{3})\,.

(22)

IV
The Fourier decomposition of the multipolar moments

One of the challenges when computing high-order PN waveforms is the treatment of hereditary integrals, such as the tails or the memory. These integrals span over the whole history of the binary, and require a model for its past evolution. Moreover, these integrals can be easily computed analytically in the case of quasicircular orbits, but the treatment of elliptic orbits is more complicated. The standard strategy is to use the periodicity (or double-periodicity) of the multipolar moments in order to express their time-evolution as a Fourier series. In general relativity, the Fourier coefficients required for the 3PN flux were computed in
Arun et al. 2008a
. These results were then improved by Loutrel and Yunes
Loutrel and Yunes 2017
, and I will follow the latter reference, adapting it to the case of ST theories.

IV.1
Moments at Newtonian order

At Newtonian order, the three eccentricities
(e_{t},e_{r},e_{\phi})
are interchangeable and denoted
e
.
Moments at Newtonian order are periodic in
\ell=n(t-t_{0})
, so they can be decomposed as a Fourier series as

\mathrm{K}_{L}(t)=\sum_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}{}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{K}}_{L}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\ell}\,,

(23)

where
\mathrm{K}_{L}
stands either for
\mathrm{I}_{L}
,
\mathrm{J}_{L}
,
\mathrm{I}^{s}_{L}
, or
E^{s}
. The Fourier coefficients are given by

{}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{K}}_{L}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\differential\ell\,\mathrm{K}_{L}(t)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}p\ell}\,.

(24)

In practice, the multipolar moments, which are known in terms of
r
,
\dot{r}
,
\phi
, and
\dot{\phi}
[see (4.6) and (4.7) of
35
], are first expressed in terms of the eccentric anomaly
u
using the Keplerian representation, and projected onto the time-independent orthonormal triad
(\bm{n}_{0},\bm{\lambda}_{0},\bm{\ell}_{0})
, which corresponds, respectively, to the components
(x,y,z)
. As illustrated in Fig.
1
:
\bm{n}_{0}
is parallel to the major axis and points from the center of the ellipse towards the periastron;
\bm{\lambda}_{0}
is parallel to the minor axis, and oriented such that
v^{i}\lambda_{0}^{i}>0
at each periastron passage;
\bm{\ell}_{0}
is orthogonal to the orbital plane, and oriented such that such that
(\bm{n}_{0},\bm{\lambda}_{0},\bm{\ell}_{0})
is a positively-oriented orthonormal basis. I will also use the corotating orthonormal basis
(\bm{n},\bm{\lambda},\bm{\ell})
defined by
\bm{n}=\cos(\phi)\bm{n}_{0}+\sin(\phi)\bm{\lambda}_{0}
,
\bm{\lambda}=-\sin(\phi)\bm{n}_{0}+\cos(\phi)\bm{\lambda}_{0}
and
\bm{\ell}=\bm{\ell}_{0}
. Since the Kepler equation reads at Newtonian order
\ell=1-e\cos u
, after a change of variables, the only integrals we will have to evaluate can in fact be reduced to Bessel functions, namely
Loutrel and Yunes 2017

J_{p}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\differential u\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(pu-x\sin u)}\,.

(25)

Moreover, following Loutrel and Yunes
Loutrel and Yunes 2017
, the expressions of the Fourier coefficients are simplified by exploiting identities between the Bessel functions and their derivatives [see their Eqs. (46-48)]. Thus, the following rules were recursively applied, valid for
x\neq 0
:

\displaystyle J_{p+n}(x)=\frac{2(p+n-1)}{x}J_{p+n-1}(x)-J_{p+n-2}(x)\qquad\qquad\text{if}\quad n\geq 2\,,

(26a)

\displaystyle J_{p-n}(x)=\frac{2(p-n+1)}{x}J_{p-n+1}(x)-J_{p-n+2}(x)\qquad\qquad\text{if}\quad n\leq 1\,,

(26b)

\displaystyle J_{p+1}(x)=\frac{p}{x}J_{p}(x)-J^{\prime}_{p}(x)\,.

(26c)

To simplify the presentation, the Fourier coefficients are normalized as

{}_{p}\widetilde{K}_{L}=\mathcal{K}_{\ell}\times{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{K}}_{L}\,,

(27)

where the generic normalization
\mathcal{K}_{\ell}
is explicitly given for each moment by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{s}_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{8\sqrt{\alpha}\zeta m\nu c^{2}x}{3(1-\zeta)}\Big[\mathcal{S}_{+}-\frac{1}{4}\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta-3\bar{\gamma}^{-1}(\mathcal{S}_{+}\bar{\beta}_{+}+\mathcal{S}_{-}\bar{\beta}_{-})\Big]\,,

(28a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{s}_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\mathcal{S}_{-}\zeta\tilde{G}\alpha^{3/2}m^{2}\nu}{c^{2}(1-\zeta)\phi_{0}x}\,,

(28b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{s}_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(\mathcal{S}_{+}-\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta)\zeta\tilde{G}^{2}\alpha^{5/2}m^{3}\nu}{c^{4}(1-\zeta)\phi_{0}x^{2}}\,,

(28c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{G}^{2}\alpha^{2}m^{3}\nu}{c^{4}\phi_{0}x^{2}}\,.

(28d)

a
\mu
m
r_{p}
r
u
v
x
y
\bm{\ell}_{0}
\bm{\lambda}_{0}
\bm{n}_{0}
\bm{n}
\bm{\lambda}

Figure 1:
Elliptical motion

Omitting the terms that vanish or that can immediately be deduced from the fact that the moments are symmetric in their indices, I find, for
p\neq 0
(the
p=0
terms will never be needed):

\displaystyle{}_{p}\widehat{E}^{s}

\displaystyle=J_{p}(ep)\,,

(29a)

\displaystyle{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}J^{\prime}_{p}(ep)\,,

(29b)

\displaystyle{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{y}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}{ep}J_{p}(ep)\,,

(29c)

\displaystyle{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{xx}

\displaystyle={}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{xx}=\frac{2}{ep}\bigg[(1-e^{2})J^{\prime}_{p}(ep)+\frac{(e^{2}-3)}{3ep}J_{p}(ep)\bigg]\,,

(29d)

\displaystyle{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{xy}

\displaystyle={}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{xy}=\frac{2\mathrm{i}\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}{ep}\bigg[\frac{1}{p}J^{\prime}_{p}(ep)-\frac{1-e^{2}}{e}J_{p}(ep)\bigg]\,,

(29e)

\displaystyle{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{yy}

\displaystyle={}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{yy}=-\frac{2}{ep}\bigg[(1-e^{2})J^{\prime}_{p}(ep)+\frac{(2e^{2}-3)}{3ep}J_{p}(ep)\bigg]\,,

(29f)

\displaystyle{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{zz}

\displaystyle={}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{zz}=\frac{2}{3p^{2}}J_{p}(ep)\,,

(29g)

where it is immediate to verify that
{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{xx}+{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{yy}+{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{zz}=0
and
{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{xx}+{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{yy}+{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{zz}=0
.

IV.2
The scalar dipole moment at 1PN

The Fourier decomposition of
\mathrm{I}^{s}_{i}
will also be required at 1PN order. For this, the decomposition will be slightly different from the Newtonian case, because the relativistic precession of the periapsis requires that the doubly periodic structure of the motion be correctly account for. Since the ellipse is precessing, the
(\bm{n}_{0},\bm{\lambda}_{0},\bm{\ell}_{0})
triad is now defined based on the osculating ellipse at current time
t
, and will remain constant throughout the past motion. Thus, at times
t^{\prime}<t
, the major and minor axes are not necessarily aligned with
(\bm{n}_{0},\bm{\lambda}_{0})
.

Following Arun
et al.

Arun et al. 2008a
, I first express
\mathrm{I}_{i}^{s}
in terms of
r
and
\phi
, and use the decomposition

\phi=K\ell+W(\ell)=k\ell+\ell+W(\ell)\,,

(30)

where, at this order,
W(\ell)=K(v-\ell)
is a
2\pi
-periodic function of
\ell
. The explicit expression of
\mathrm{I}_{i}^{s}
is solely composed of terms proportional to
\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\phi}
, so it is immediate to factor out the dependence in
k\ell
, namely

\mathrm{I}_{i}^{s}=\sum_{m\in\{-1,1\}}{}^{m}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{i}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}mk\ell}=\mathcal{I}_{1}\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{m\in\{-1,1\}}{}^{m}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{i}\,\mathrm{e}^{imk\ell}\,,

(31)

where the normalization
\mathcal{I}_{1}
is the same as for the Newtonian case. The residual
{}^{m}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{i}
terms can then be written as a sum of terms proportional to
\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}[\ell+W(\ell)]}
, which are all
2\pi
-periodic in
\ell
because
W(\ell)
is. Thus, it can be decomposed into a Fourier series. The final expansion reflects the double periodicity of the motion, and reads

\mathrm{I}_{i}^{s}=\mathcal{I}_{1}\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{m\in\{-1,1\}}\sum_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}\,{}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{i}^{s}\mathrm{e}^{i(mk+p)\ell}\,,

(32)

where

\displaystyle\,{}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{i}^{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\!\!\differential\ell\ {}^{m}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{i}^{s}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}p\ell}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\!\!\differential u\,\frac{\differential\ell}{\differential u}\,{}^{m}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{i}^{s}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}p(u-e_{t}\sin u)}\,,

(33)

and where we recall from (
III
) that
\differential\ell/\differential u=1-e_{t}\cos u+\mathcal{O}(c^{-4})
.
In order to explicitly compute
{}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{i}^{s}
, recall that
{}^{m}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{i}(t)
only contains terms proportional to
\mathrm{e}^{\pm i[\ell+W(\ell)]}
, and use the 1PN-accurate relation

\ell+W(\ell)=v+k(w+e_{t}\sin u)+\mathcal{O}(c^{-4})\,.

(34)

The exponential is PN-expanded to find

\mathrm{e}^{\pm i[\ell+W(\ell)]}=\mathrm{e}^{\pm iv}\Big(1\pm\mathrm{i}k[\tilde{w}+e_{t}\sin u]+\mathcal{O}(c^{-4})\Big)\,,

(35)

where I have replaced at this order
w
by
\tilde{w}
, and where
k
can be replaced by its leading order expression in terms of
x
and
e_{t}
. The exponential term is easily computed using trigonometric functions, namely

\mathrm{e}^{\pm iv}=\frac{\cos u-e_{\phi}\pm\mathrm{i}\sqrt{1-e_{\phi}^{2}}\sin u}{1-e_{\phi}\cos u}\,,

(36)

where
e_{\phi}
should then be replaced in terms of its 1PN expression in terms of
x
and
e_{t}
.
After all replacements are made, I obtain a 1PN accurate expression in terms only of
x
,
e_{t}
and
u
. From the structure of this expression, it appears that the Fourier coefficients can only be computed by introducing, on top of the Bessel functions, the real-valued
3
3

3

By a
u^{\prime}=-u
variable change, it is clear that
\Theta_{-p}^{-q}(e_{t})=-\Theta_{p}^{q}(e_{t})
, by oddness of
\arctan
. This also means that
\Theta_{p}^{q}(e_{t})=\Theta_{p}^{q}(e_{t})^{*}
, so
\Theta_{p}^{q}(e_{t})\in\mathbb{R}
.
special function

\displaystyle\Theta_{p}^{q}(e_{t})

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{i}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\differential u\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}[(p+q)u-pe_{t}\sin u]}\tilde{w}(u,e_{t})\,,

(37)

which is not entirely novel since it appears in a different form in (68) of
Loutrel and Yunes 2017
.
Finally, following
Arun et al. 2008a
;
Loutrel and Yunes 2017
, it is useful for presentation purposes to expand the Fourier coefficients in the variables
x
and
\nu
, so as to present coefficients that depend only on the eccentricities. The decomposition is denoted:

\,{}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{i}^{s}=\,{}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{i}^{s,00}+x\Big[\,{}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{i}^{s,01}+\nu\,{}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{i}^{s,11}\Big]+\mathcal{O}(x^{2})\,.

(38)

First, I found that
\forall p\in\mathbb{Z},\,{}^{\pm 1}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{z}^{s}=0
, and that the following relation holds

{}^{\pm 1}_{\,\,\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{y}^{s}=(\mp\mathrm{i}){}^{\pm 1}_{\,\,\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}\,.

(39)

Thus, recalling the definition (
18
) for the recurrent parameter
\mathcal{B}
, all the information is contained in the following expressions for the
x
-component:

\displaystyle\,{}^{\pm 1}_{\,\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,{00}}_{x}

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{\sqrt{1-e_{t}^{2}}}{2pe_{t}}J_{p}(pe_{t})+\frac{1}{2p}J^{\prime}_{p}(pe_{t})\,,

(40a)

\displaystyle\,{}^{\pm 1}_{\,\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,{11}}_{x}

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{14+e_{t}^{2}}{30pe_{t}\sqrt{1-e_{t}^{2}}}J_{p}(pe_{t})+\frac{7}{15p}J^{\prime}_{p}(pe_{t})\,,

(40b)

\displaystyle\,{}^{\pm 1}_{\,\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,{01}}_{x}

\displaystyle=J_{p}(pe_{t})\Bigg[\mp\frac{\mathrm{i}\mathcal{B}}{pe_{t}}+(1-e_{t}^{2})\,\frac{\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{-}+\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}-\delta(\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{+}+\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{-})-\frac{1}{4}\bar{\gamma}(\mathcal{S}_{-}-2\mathcal{S}_{+}\delta)}{e_{t}\bar{\gamma}\mathcal{S}_{-}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{\mathrm{i}\mathcal{B}}{p^{2}e_{t}\sqrt{1-\smash{e_{t}}^{2}}}\mp\frac{\sqrt{1-\smash{e_{t}}^{2}}\mathcal{S}_{+}\delta}{10p\mathcal{S}_{-}e_{t}}\mp\frac{39+10\bar{\gamma}+20\bar{(}\beta_{+}-\bar{\beta}_{-}\delta)+e_{t}^{2}(21+10\bar{\gamma})}{60pe_{t}\sqrt{1-\smash{e_{t}}^{2}}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle+J^{\prime}_{p}(pe_{t})\Bigg[\pm\frac{\mathrm{i}\mathcal{B}}{p^{2}\left(1-\smash{e_{t}}^{2}\right)}-\frac{\mathcal{S}_{+}\delta}{10p\mathcal{S}_{-}}-\frac{39+10\bar{\gamma}+20\bar{(}\beta_{+}-\bar{\beta}_{-}\delta)-e_{t}^{2}(99+50\bar{\gamma})}{60p(1-\smash{e_{t}}^{2})}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{\mathrm{i}\mathcal{B}}{p(1-e_{t})^{2}}\pm\sqrt{1-\smash{e_{t}}^{2}}\,\frac{\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{-}+\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}-\delta(\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{+}+\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{-})-\frac{1}{4}\bar{\gamma}(\mathcal{S}_{-}-2\mathcal{S}_{+}\delta)}{\bar{\gamma}\mathcal{S}_{-}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\mp\frac{\mathcal{B}}{8(1-e_{t}^{2})}\Bigg[e_{t}\left(1\mp\sqrt{1-\smash{e_{t}}^{2}}\right)\Theta_{p}^{+2}(e_{t})+e_{t}\left(1\pm\sqrt{1-\smash{e_{t}}^{2}}\right)\Theta_{p}^{-2}(e_{t})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-2\left(1+e_{t}^{2}\mp\sqrt{1-\smash{e_{t}}^{2}}\right)\Theta_{p}^{+1}(e_{t})-2\left(1+e_{t}^{2}\pm\sqrt{1-\smash{e_{t}}^{2}}\right)\Theta_{p}^{-1}(e_{t})+6e_{t}\,\Theta_{p}^{0}(e_{t})\Bigg]\,.

(40c)

It is easy to verify that,

\displaystyle\,{}_{p}

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}\equiv\mathrm{e}^{+\mathrm{i}k\ell}\left({}^{+1}_{\,\,\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}\right)+\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}k\ell}\left({}^{-1}_{\,\,\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}\right)\underset{c\rightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}\,{}^{+1}_{\,\,\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s,00}+\,{}^{-1}_{\,\,\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s,00}=\frac{1}{p}J^{\prime}_{p}(pe_{t})\,,

which is indeed the expression I found in the Newtonian case — the same holds for the
y
-component.
Finally, the following identities, valid for any
(p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, will prove very useful
4
4

4

The proof goes as follows. Performing an explicit summation over the contracted indices, I find that
\forall(m,s)\in\{-1,1\}^{2}
,

\displaystyle(m+s)\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{i}^{s}\right)\left(\!{}^{m}_{\,\,q}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{i}^{s}\right)

\displaystyle=(m+s)(1-ms)\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}\right)\left(\!{}^{m}_{\,\,q}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}\right)\,,

\displaystyle(m+s)\,\epsilon_{ijk}\!\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{j}^{s}\right)\left(\!{}^{m}_{\,\,q}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{k}^{s}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}(m+s)(m-s)\,\epsilon_{ixy}\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}\right)\left(\!{}^{m}_{\,\,q}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}\right)\,,

where I have used (
39
) and the fact that the
z
component of the dipole vanishes.
But the right hand sides of the previous equations vanish identically for any allowed values of
m
and
s
, which concludes the proof.

in Sec.
VI
:

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\{-1,1\}\\
s\in\{-1,1\}\end{subarray}}(m+s)\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{i}^{s}\right)\left(\!{}^{m}_{\,\,q}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{i}^{s}\right)=0\,,

(41a)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\{-1,1\}\\
s\in\{-1,1\}\end{subarray}}(m+s)\,\epsilon_{ijk}\!\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{j}^{s}\right)\left(\!{}^{m}_{\,\,q}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{k}^{s}\right)=0\,.

(41b)

IV.3
Special functions

The Fourier coefficients computed previously feature some special functions. The most common ones are the Bessel functions, which are well known, but the new special function
\Theta_{p}^{q}(e_{t})
deserves some attention. For
p+q\neq 0
, it behaves as
\Theta_{p}^{q}(e_{t})=\mathcal{O}(e_{t}^{|p+q|})
when as
e_{t}\rightarrow 0
, whereas
\Theta_{p}^{-p}(e_{t})=\mathcal{O}(e_{t}^{2})
. It is therefore useful to define the renormalized function

\widetilde{\Theta}_{p}^{q}(e_{t})=\begin{cases}e_{t}^{-2}\Theta_{p}^{q}(e_{t})&\quad\text{if}\ p+q=0\\
e_{t}^{-|p+q|}\Theta_{p}^{q}(e_{t})&\quad\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\,,

(42)

such that
\tilde{\Theta}^{p}_{q}(0)
is always finite and nonzero (except for
p=q=0
, since
\Theta^{0}_{0}(e_{t})
is identically zero).
These special functions only appear in the unnormalized enhancement functions
\rho_{1}^{s,01}(e_{t})
and
\tilde{\rho}_{1}^{s,01}(e_{t})
[see Eqs. (
VI.4
)], which means that they only appear in the normalized enhancement functions
\alpha_{1}^{s}(e_{t})
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{1}^{s}(e_{t})
[see Eqs. (
VI.4
)]. In Fig.
2
, I have plotted the exact values of all the special functions which are needed to evaluate these enhancement functions, along with the case
p=0
, which was never needed but is nonetheless instructive.

Figure 2:
Numerical plots of the
\widetilde{\Theta}_{p}^{q}(e_{t})
required for this computation, as well as the special case
p=0
.

In view of obtaining fully analytical results, it is also useful to control the small eccentricity expansion of these special functions. First, recall that the Bessel function and its derivative can be expanded for
p\neq 0
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{p}(pe_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!(n+|p|)!}\Big(\frac{|p|e_{t}}{2}\Big)^{2n+|p|}\,,

(43)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{\prime}_{p}(pe_{t})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sgn}(p)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}(2n+|p|)}{2(n!)(n+|p|)!}\Big(\frac{|p|e_{t}}{2}\Big)^{2n+|p|-1}\,,

(44)

where I have introduced the sign function
\mathrm{sgn}(p)=1
if
p>0
,
\mathrm{sgn}(p)=-1
if
p<0
.

The Taylor expansion of
\Theta_{p}^{q}(e_{t})
as
e_{t}\rightarrow 0
can be easily obtained at any order for given values of
p
and
q
(e.g. with
Mathematica
), but its general term is very cumbersome. Here, I will only present the results which are required to obtain the expansions of the normalized enhancement functions
\alpha_{1}^{s}(e_{t})
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{1}^{s}(e_{t})
at tenth order in the eccentricity [see Eqs. (
112
) and App.
C
]. The corresponding expansions are:

\displaystyle\Theta_{1}^{-2}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}e_{t}-\frac{23}{384}e_{t}^{5}-\frac{181}{4608}e_{t}^{7}-\frac{43093}{1474560}e_{t}^{9}+\mathcal{O}(e_{t}^{11})

(45a)

\displaystyle\Theta_{1}^{-1}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}e_{t}^{2}-\frac{1}{16}e_{t}^{4}-\frac{59}{1152}e_{t}^{6}-\frac{203}{6144}e_{t}^{8}-\frac{58183}{2457600}e_{t}^{10}+\mathcal{O}(e_{t}^{12})

(45b)
