V
Passage to the center-of-mass frame at 2.5PN order

In order to use the quasi-Keplerian parametrization, but also to yield simple and unambiguous results, the fluxes will be presented in the CM frame. However, the transformations from a general frame to the CM frame (including both conservative and dissipative contributions) are currently known only up to 2PN in scalar tensor theory
Bernard et al. 2022
, but the computation of the angular momentum flux requires these laws at 2.5PN. This is the object the present section. Importantly, it was recently rediscovered (in general relativity) that it is vital to define the CM frame as the barycenter of both the matter and radiation
Blanchet et al. 2024
, the latter entering at high PN order as nonlocal integrals over the radiation escaping at infinity. Previous results
Gopakumar et al. 1997
;
Leibovich et al. 2023
that ignored the radiation contribution were therefore erroneous. In this section, I will therefore be careful to include these contributions.

V.1
Flux balance laws for the linear momentum and position of the center of mass

Due the the symmetries of Minkowski spacetime, the ten Poincaré invariants of the binary system (its energy
E
, angular momentum
J^{i}
, linear momentum
P^{i}
and CM position
G^{i}
) are conserved in the absence of gravitational radiation. When including the radiation, these quantities are only approximately conserved, and are described by flux-balance laws. For the energy and angular momentum, these are described in
47
for the case of ST theory, whereas the linear momentum,
P^{i}
, and the position of the CM,
G^{i}
, satisfy

\displaystyle\frac{\differential P^{i}}{\differential t}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{F}_{\bm{P}}^{i}-\mathcal{F}_{s,\bm{P}}^{i}\,,

(46a)

\displaystyle\frac{\differential G^{i}}{\differential t}

\displaystyle=P^{i}-\mathcal{F}_{\bm{G}}^{i}-\mathcal{F}_{s,\bm{G}}^{i}\,.

(46b)

The tensorial fluxes,
\mathcal{F}_{\bm{P}}^{i}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\bm{G}}^{i}
, are given (up to global factor
\phi_{0}
) in terms of the radiative moments by (4.12b-4.15b) of
Compère et al. 2020
, and the scalar fluxes are defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{i}_{s,\bm{P}}

\displaystyle=\frac{c^{4}\phi_{0}}{16\pi G}\int\differential^{2}\!S_{j}\,\Lambda_{\phi}^{ij}\,,

(47a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{i}_{s,\bm{G}}

\displaystyle=\frac{c^{3}\phi_{0}}{16\pi G}\int\differential^{2}\!S_{j}\,\Big[x^{i}\Lambda_{\phi}^{0j}-r\Lambda_{\phi}^{ij}\Big]\,,

(47b)

where
\int\differential^{2}\!S_{j}
denotes integration over the sphere. When taking the
r\rightarrow 0
limit, only the asymptotic expression of
\Lambda_{\phi}
are needed, namely

\Lambda^{\mu\nu}_{\phi}=(3+\omega_{0})\left(\eta^{\mu\alpha}\eta^{\nu\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\mu\nu}\eta^{\alpha\beta}\right)\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\psi+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{r^{3}}\right)\,.

(48)

Discarding the
\mathcal{O}(1/r^{3})
term which is contains at least cubic terms in the multipolar moments,
5

and plugging this expression into (
47
), I find

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{i}_{s,\bm{P}}

\displaystyle=\frac{c^{2}(3+2\omega_{0})\phi_{0}r^{2}}{16\pi G}\int\differential\Omega\,n_{i}\dot{\psi}^{2}\,,

(49a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{i}_{s,\bm{G}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{c^{3}(3+2\omega_{0})\phi_{0}r^{3}}{16\pi G}\int\differential\Omega\,\Bigg[n_{j}\partial_{i}\psi\partial_{j}\psi+\frac{n_{i}n_{j}}{c}\dot{\psi}\partial_{j}\psi-\frac{1}{2}n^{i}\left(\partial_{k}\psi\partial_{k}\psi-\frac{1}{c^{2}}\dot{\psi}^{2}\right)\Bigg]\,,

(49b)

where
\int\differential\Omega
denotes integration over angles. Finally, replacing
\psi
by its multipolar expansion (
10b
) yields the final expressions
5
5

5

Just as in
Thorne 1980
, the uncontrolled
O(R^{-2})
subleading term of the asymptotic multipolar expansion is assumed not to contribute. This assumption was rigorously proven in
Compère et al. 2020
within the Bondi-Sachs framework in the case of GR, and I will assume without proof that the same arguments hold in the scalar sector.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{i}_{s,\bm{P}}

\displaystyle=G\phi_{0}(3+2\omega_{0})\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{2}{c^{2\ell+3}\ell!(2\ell+3)!!}\overset{(1)}{\mathcal{U}}{\!}{}_{L}^{s}\overset{(1)}{\mathcal{U}}{}_{iL}^{s}\,,

(50a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{i}_{s,\bm{G}}

\displaystyle=G\phi_{0}(3+2\omega_{0})\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{\ell+2}{c^{2\ell+3}\ell!(2\ell+3)!!}\Big(\overset{(1)}{\mathcal{U}}{\!}{}_{L}^{s}{\mathcal{U}}_{iL}^{s}-\frac{\ell}{\ell+2}\overset{(1)}{\mathcal{U}}{\!}{}_{iL}^{s}{\mathcal{U}}_{L}^{s}\Big)\,.

(50b)

Note that, since the monopole is constant at Newtonian order and only acquires time-dependent contributions at subleading 1PN order,
\differential\mathcal{U}^{s}/\differential t
is actually a 1PN quantity. Thus, the leading contributions to the scalar fluxes for
P^{i}
and
G^{i}
are of order
\mathcal{O}(1/c^{5})
, and read

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{i}_{s,\bm{P}}

\displaystyle=\frac{G\phi_{0}(3+2\omega_{0})}{c^{5}}\bigg[\frac{2}{3}\frac{\overset{(1)}{E}{}^{s}}{\phi_{0}}\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}_{i}^{s}+\frac{2}{15}\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{j}\overset{(3)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{ij}\bigg]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{7}}\right)\,,

(51a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{i}_{s,\bm{G}}

\displaystyle=\frac{G\phi_{0}(3+2\omega_{0})}{c^{5}}\bigg[\frac{2}{3}\frac{\overset{(1)}{E}{}^{s}}{\phi_{0}}\overset{(1)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}_{i}^{s}+\frac{1}{5}\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{j}\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{ij}-\frac{1}{15}\overset{(1)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{j}\overset{(3)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{ij}\bigg]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{7}}\right)\,.

(51b)

The expression (
50b
) of the scalar flux of CM position is analogous to the tensorial flux of CM position found by
Kozameh et al. 2018
;
Compère et al. 2020
, rather than the one obtained by
Blanchet and Faye 2019
. For our purposes, both expressions are equivalent, because they differ by a total time derivative, see the discussion in Sec. V of
Blanchet et al. 2024
.

V.2
Passage to the center of mass at 2.5PN order

For the clarity of the discussion, the linear momentum and position of the CM are first decomposed into different PN contributions, namely

\displaystyle\bm{P}

\displaystyle=\bm{P}^{\text{N}}+\bm{P}^{\text{1PN}}+\bm{P}^{\text{1.5PN}}+\bm{P}^{\text{2PN}}+\bm{P}^{\text{2.5PN}}+\mathcal{O}(1/c^{6})\,,

(52a)

\displaystyle\bm{G}

\displaystyle=\bm{G}^{\text{N}}+\bm{G}^{\text{1PN}}+\bm{G}^{\text{1.5PN}}+\bm{G}^{\text{2PN}}+\bm{G}^{\text{2.5PN}}+\mathcal{O}(1/c^{6})\,.

(52b)

Before this work, the even (conservative) contributions to
\bm{G}
and
\bm{P}
were derived in Ref.
Bernard 2019
. The 1.5PN dissipative contributions have been previously computed in order to obtain (A.2) of
Bernard et al. 2022
, but were not published. They read:

\displaystyle G_{i}^{\text{1.5PN}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{G}\alpha m^{2}\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}(2\nu-\delta-1)v_{12}^{i}}{3c^{3}}\,,

(53a)

\displaystyle P_{i}^{\text{1.5PN}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\tilde{G}^{2}\alpha^{2}m^{3}\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}(1+\delta-3\nu-\nu\delta)n_{12}^{i}}{3c^{3}r_{12}^{2}}\,,

(53b)

and are defined such that
\differential G^{i}/\differential t=P_{i}+\mathcal{O}(1/c^{5})
and
\differential P^{i}/\differential t=\mathcal{O}(1/c^{5})
when using the full 2PN equations of motion, including both conservative and dissipative contributions. The latter were first computed in
Mirshekari and Will 2013
, but the 1.5PN term is given in a more explicit form in (A.1a) of
35
. At 2PN order, the equation for the passage to the CM is then obtained by solving
G^{i}=\mathcal{O}(1/c^{5})
, and is given by (A.2) of
35
.

I am now going to obtain the 2.5PN-accurate equation for the passage to the CM frame, but without needing to determine
\bm{G}^{\text{2.5PN}}
in a general frame
Blanchet
. At 2.5PN order enter the associated fluxes for
\bm{P}
and
\bm{G}
, so the CM frame (associated to both the matter content and gravitational waves) will now be generically defined by the condition
Blanchet et al. 2024

\displaystyle G_{i}+\Gamma_{i}+\Gamma_{i}^{s}=0\,,

(54)

where the radiation contribution is described by

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{t}\!\!\!\!\differential t^{\prime}\mathcal{F}_{\bm{G}}^{i}(t^{\prime})+\int_{-\infty}^{t}\!\!\differential t^{\prime}\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{t^{\prime}}\!\!\differential t^{\prime\prime}\mathcal{F}_{\bm{P}}^{i}(t^{\prime\prime})\,,

(55a)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i}^{s}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{t}\!\!\!\!\differential t^{\prime}\mathcal{F}_{s,\bm{G}}^{i}(t^{\prime})+\int_{-\infty}^{t}\!\!\differential t^{\prime}\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{t^{\prime}}\!\!\differential t^{\prime\prime}\mathcal{F}_{s,\bm{P}}^{i}(t^{\prime\prime})\,.

(55b)

It will also prove useful to introduce

\displaystyle\Pi_{i}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{t}\!\!\differential t^{\prime}\mathcal{F}_{\bm{P}}^{i}(t^{\prime})\,,

(56a)

\displaystyle\Pi_{i}^{s}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{t}\!\!\differential t^{\prime}\mathcal{F}_{s,\bm{P}}^{i}(t^{\prime})\,,

(56b)

such that
P_{i}+\Pi_{i}+\Pi_{i}^{s}=0
is satisfied in the CM frame (in fact, this equation is the
definion
of a center-of-inertia frame).
Note that at 2.5PN, the tensor nonlocal quantities
\Gamma_{i}
and
\Pi_{i}
do not contribute. Now, using the decomposition (
52
), one can rewrite the balance equations for the linear momentum (
46a
) as

\displaystyle\frac{\differential P_{i}^{\text{2.5PN}}}{\differential t}=-\bigg(\frac{\differential P_{i}^{\text{N}}}{\differential t}+\frac{\differential P_{i}^{\text{1PN}}}{\differential t}+\frac{\differential P_{i}^{\text{1.5N}}}{\differential t}+\frac{\differential P_{i}^{\text{2PN}}}{\differential t}+\mathcal{F}^{i}_{\bm{P}}+\mathcal{F}^{i}_{s,\bm{P}}\bigg)\,.

(57)

Since
{\differential P_{i}^{\text{N}}}/{\differential t}=\mathcal{O}(1/c^{2})
, all quantities on the right hand side of (
57
) can be rewritten with 2.5PN accuracy in terms of the CM variables with the sole knowledge of the
1.5
PN-accurate formula for the passage to the CM frame, which is given by (3.6-8) of
Bernard 2019
for the conservative piece and (A.2a) of
Bernard et al. 2022
for the dissipative piece. By construction, the right-hand side will be of order
\mathcal{O}(1/c^{5})
in the CM frame.

For any quantity
\mathcal{Z}
expressed in terms of
(\bm{y_{1}},\bm{y_{2}},\bm{v_{1}},\bm{v}_{2})
, I will now define the related quantity
\left(\mathcal{Z}\right)_{\text{CM}}
, which depends only on
(x^{i},v^{i})
and is obtained by applying to
\mathcal{Z}
the formulas for the passage to the CM (e.g.
y_{1}^{i}=...
) at the relevant order. Note that in the following, I will often be able to control
\left(\mathcal{Z}\right)_{\text{CM}}
without controlling the full expression of
\mathcal{Z}
.

The quantity I am now interested in obtaining is
\left(P_{i}^{\text{2.5PN}}\right)_{\text{CM}}
, expressed at leading 2.5PN order. To this end, the equation which should be solved reads

\left.\frac{\differential\left(P_{i}^{\text{2.5PN}}\right)_{\text{CM}}}{\differential t}\right|_{a_{i}^{\text{CM},\text{N}}}=\Big(\text{RHS of \eqref{eq:balanceLawPiReshuffled}}\Big)_{\text{CM}}\,,

(58)

where
RHS
denotes the “right hand side” of the equation, and where I have indicated that only the Newtonian relative acceleration in the CM frame
a_{i}^{\text{CM,N}}
is needed when taking the time derivative. This is easily solved in the CM frame, and the solution reads:

\displaystyle\left(P_{i}^{\text{2.5PN}}\right)_{\text{CM}}

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{p_{1}}{r^{3}}+p_{2}\frac{(nv)^{2}}{r^{2}}+p_{3}\frac{v^{2}}{r^{2}}\right]n^{i}+p_{4}\frac{(nv)v^{i}}{r^{2}}\,,

(59)

where the coefficients
p_{n}
are given in App.
B
. Repeating this process for the CM balance equation (
46b
) leads to

\displaystyle\frac{\differential G_{i}^{\text{2.5PN}}}{\differential t}

\displaystyle=P_{i}^{\text{N}}+P_{i}^{\text{1PN}}+P_{i}^{\text{1.5N}}+P_{i}^{\text{2PN}}+P_{i}^{\text{2.5PN}}-\bigg(\frac{\differential G_{i}^{\text{N}}}{\differential t}+\frac{\differential G_{i}^{\text{1PN}}}{\differential t}+\frac{\differential G_{i}^{\text{1.5N}}}{\differential t}+\frac{\differential G_{i}^{\text{2PN}}}{\differential t}+\mathcal{F}^{i}_{\bm{G}}+\mathcal{F}^{i}_{s,\bm{G}}\bigg)\,.

(60)

Since
P_{i}^{\text{N}}-\differential G_{i}^{\text{N}}/\differential t=\mathcal{O}(1/c^{2})
, and since
\left(P_{i}^{\text{2.5PN}}\right)_{\text{CM}}
has been computed in the previous step, one can again easily express the right hand side of (
60
) in terms of the CM variables using solely the
1.5
PN-accurate formula for the passage to the CM. Again, by construction, the right-hand side is thus of order
\mathcal{O}(1/c^{5})
in the CM frame, and one is left to solve

\left.\frac{\differential\left(G_{i}^{\text{2.5PN}}\right)_{\text{CM}}}{\differential t}\right|_{a_{i}^{\text{CM},\text{N}}}=\Big(\text{RHS of \eqref{eq:balanceLawGiReshuffled}}\Big)_{\text{CM}}\,,

(61)

Again, this is easily solved for in the CM frame, and I find:

\displaystyle\left(G_{i}^{\text{2.5PN}}\right)_{\text{CM}}

\displaystyle=q_{1}\frac{(nv)n^{i}}{r}+\left[\frac{q_{2}}{r}+q_{3}v^{2}\right]v^{i}\,,

(62)

where the coefficients
q_{n}
are given in App.
B
. With this expression, I am now in a position to solve for
y_{1}^{i}
with 2.5PN accuracy in the equation

\displaystyle G_{i}^{\text{N}}+\Big(G_{i}^{\text{1PN}}+G_{i}^{\text{1.5PN}}+G_{i}^{\text{2PN}}+G_{i}^{\text{2.5PN}}+\Gamma_{i}+\Gamma_{i}^{s}\Big)_{\text{CM}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{6}}\right)\,,

(63)

where
G_{i}^{\text{N}}=m_{1}y_{1}^{i}+m_{2}y_{2}^{i}=my_{1}^{i}-m_{2}rn^{i}
.
This yields the final expression

\displaystyle y_{1}^{i}

\displaystyle=\left(X_{2}+\nu\mathcal{P}\right)x^{i}+\nu\mathcal{Q}v^{i}+\mathcal{R}_{i}\,

(64a)

\displaystyle y_{2}^{i}

\displaystyle=\left(-X_{1}+\nu\mathcal{P}\right)x^{i}+\nu\mathcal{Q}v^{i}+\mathcal{R}_{i}\,,

(64b)

where
X_{1}=m_{1}/m=(1+\delta)/2
and
X_{2}=m_{2}/m=(1-\delta)/2
. The
\mathcal{R}_{i}
corresponds to the contribution arising from the radiation (in line with the conventions of Ref.
Blanchet et al. 2024
), and simply reads

\mathcal{R}^{i}=-\frac{\Gamma_{i}^{s}}{m}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{7}}\right)\,,

(65)

where it should be noted that
\Gamma_{i}^{s}
is intrinsically a 2.5PN quantity, i.e.
\Gamma_{i}^{s}=\mathcal{O}\left(c^{-5}\right)
.
Conversely, the
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
contributions are the contributions arising from matter [i.e. what one would find if one solved
G^{i}=0
]. They are decomposed as

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{\text{1PN}}+\mathcal{P}_{\text{1.5PN}}+\mathcal{P}_{\text{2PN}}+\mathcal{P}_{\text{2.5PN}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{6}}\right)\,,

(66a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{\text{1PN}}+\mathcal{Q}_{\text{1.5PN}}+\mathcal{Q}_{\text{2PN}}+\mathcal{Q}_{\text{2.5PN}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{6}}\right)\,,

(66b)

where the conservative contributions
\mathcal{P}_{\text{1PN}}
,
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{1PN}}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\text{2PN}}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{2PN}}
, are given by (3.7) and (3.8) of
Bernard 2019
, and where the dissipative contributions read
6
6

6

See also (A.2) of
Bernard et al. 2022
for the 1.5PN piece, where the 1.5PN contributions of
y_{1}^{i}
and
v_{1}^{i}
are expressed with a less compact choice of ST variables.

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\text{1.5PN}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(67a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\text{1.5PN}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\tilde{G}\alpha m\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}(\mathcal{S}_{+}+\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta)}{3c^{3}}\,,

(67b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\text{2.5PN}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\tilde{G}\alpha m)^{2}\,(nv)}{15c^{5}r^{2}}\Big[10\bar{\beta}_{-}-5\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}+24\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}\nu+\delta(5\bar{\gamma}-10\bar{\beta}_{+}-5\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2})+26\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\nu\delta\Big]\,,

(67c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\text{2.5PN}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{G}\alpha mv^{2}}{15c^{5}}\Big[5\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}+\delta(12+5\bar{\gamma}+5\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2})-23\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}\nu-17\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\nu\delta\Big]

\displaystyle+\frac{(\tilde{G}\alpha m)^{2}}{15c^{5}r}\bigg[-10\bar{\beta}_{-}-5\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}+\delta(-24-15\bar{\gamma}+10\bar{\beta}_{+}-5\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\ \,+\nu\Big(160\bar{\gamma}^{-1}\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}(\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{+}+\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{-})-18\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}\Big)+28\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\nu\delta\bigg]\,.

(67d)

Taking the time derivative of (
64
), using the 2.5PN-accurate acceleration
Mirshekari and Will 2013
;
Bernard 2019
;
Bernard et al. 2022
, as well as the identity
\differential\Gamma^{s}_{i}/\differential t=\Pi^{s}_{i}+\mathcal{F}^{i}_{s,\bm{G}}
, one obtains the expressions for the passage to the CM for
v_{1}^{i}
and
v_{2}^{i}
.
For the reader’s convenience, the expressions of
\mathcal{F}^{i}_{s,\bm{P}}
(which enter the definition of
\bm{\Pi}^{s}
) and of
\mathcal{F}^{i}_{s,\bm{G}}
(which enters the definitions of
\bm{\Pi}^{s}
and
\bm{\Gamma}^{s}
) are given at leading order and in the CM frame as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{i}_{s,\bm{P}}=\frac{8(\tilde{G}\alpha m)^{3}m\nu^{2}\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}}{15c^{5}r^{4}}\Bigg[\Big(20\bar{\gamma}^{-1}(\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{+}+\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{-})-8\mathcal{S}_{+}+3\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta\Big)(nv)n^{i}+2\big(\mathcal{S}_{+}-\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta\big)v^{i}\Bigg]\,,

(68a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{i}_{s,\bm{G}}=\frac{4(\tilde{G}\alpha m)^{2}m\nu^{2}\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}}{15c^{5}r^{2}}\Bigg[(\mathcal{S}_{+}-\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta)\bigg(\frac{2\tilde{G}\alpha m}{r}-3(nv)^{2}+3v^{2}\bigg)n^{i}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad-\Big(40\bar{\gamma}^{-1}(\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{-}+\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{-})-12\mathcal{S}_{+}+2\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta\Big)(nv)v^{i}\Bigg]\,.

(68b)

Note that only the 1.5PN-accurate formulas for the passage to the CM frame were required when deriving the 2.5PN equations of motion in the CM frame, so previous results
Mirshekari and Will 2013
;
Bernard et al. 2022
are unaffected by the inclusion of the radiation contributions.

V.3
Expression of
\Gamma_{i}^{s}
and
\Pi_{i}^{s}
for an elliptic orbit

The appearance of nonlocal terms in the formula for the passage to the CM frame introduces a practical difficulty, because it depends on the whole past history of the binary. In this section, I will show that assuming the two-body system has eternally been isolated makes it possible to “localize” these terms, in a similar way to other nonlocal effects like tails or memory. I will consider the case of a binary on an elliptic orbit (only the leading Newtonian order is required here), and discuss its reduction it to the special case of circular orbits at the end.

Since these quantities are to be computed only at leading order, I assume that the motion has been eternally elliptic, where the orbital parameters adiabatically evolve due to radiation reaction. Thus, at this level of accuracy, the semimajor axis
a
and the eccentricity
e
asymptote, respectively, to
+\infty
and
1
in the infinite past. The fluxes of linear momentum (
51a
) and CM position (
51b
) are controlled at any point in the past, which I subdivide into an orbit-averaged contribution and a zero-average oscillating contribution, namely
\mathcal{F}_{s,\bm{P}}^{i}=\langle\mathcal{F}_{s,\bm{P}}^{i}\rangle+\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s,\bm{P}}^{i}
and
\mathcal{F}_{s,\bm{G}}^{i}=\langle\mathcal{F}_{s,\bm{G}}^{i}\rangle+\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s,\bm{G}}^{i}
. Since only the leading-order contribution in
\Pi_{i}^{s}
and
\Gamma_{i}^{s}
are required, I will consider that the motion is Keplerian, up to leading-order radiation reaction terms. This leads to the decomposition
\Pi^{s}_{i}=\Pi^{s,\text{DC}}_{i}+\Pi^{s,\text{AC}}_{i}
and
\Gamma^{s}_{i}=\Gamma^{s,\text{DC}}_{i}+\Gamma^{s,\text{AC}}_{i}
, where

\displaystyle\Pi_{i}^{s,\text{DC}}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{t}\differential t^{\prime}\langle\mathcal{F}_{s,\bm{P}}^{i}\rangle(t^{\prime})\,,\qquad\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i}^{s,\text{DC}}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{t}\differential t^{\prime}\langle\mathcal{F}_{s,\bm{G}}^{i}\rangle(t^{\prime})+\int_{-\infty}^{t}\!\!\differential t^{\prime}\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{t^{\prime}}\!\!\differential t^{\prime\prime}\left\langle\mathcal{F}_{s,\bm{P}}^{i}\right\rangle(t^{\prime\prime})\,,

\displaystyle\Pi_{i}^{s,\text{AC}}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{t}\differential t^{\prime}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s,\bm{P}}^{i}(t^{\prime})\,,\qquad\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i}^{s,\text{AC}}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{t}\differential t^{\prime}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s,\bm{G}}^{i}(t^{\prime})+\int_{-\infty}^{t}\!\!\differential t^{\prime}\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{t^{\prime}}\!\!\differential t^{\prime\prime}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s,\bm{P}}^{i}(t^{\prime\prime})\,.

(69)

V.3.1
The AC part

I will first consider the AC contribution. The oscillatory part of the flux can be written in terms of the Fourier decomposition of the source moments (see Sec.
IV
), and reads

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{i}_{s,\bm{P}}

\displaystyle=\frac{G\phi_{0}(3+2\omega_{0})}{c^{5}}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q)\ell}\bigg[\frac{2(\mathrm{i}pn)(\mathrm{i}qn)^{2}}{3\phi_{0}}\Big({}_{p}\widetilde{E}^{s}\Big)\Big({}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{i}\Big)+\frac{2(\mathrm{i}pn)^{2}(\mathrm{i}qn)^{3}}{15}\Big({}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{j}\Big)\Big({}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{ij}\Big)\bigg]\,,

(70a)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{i}_{s,\bm{G}}

\displaystyle=\frac{G\phi_{0}(3+2\omega_{0})}{c^{5}}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q)\ell}\bigg[\frac{2}{3}\frac{(\mathrm{i}pn)(\mathrm{i}qn)}{\phi_{0}}\Big({}_{p}\widetilde{E}^{s}\Big)\Big({}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{i}\Big)+\Big(\frac{(\mathrm{i}pn)^{2}(\mathrm{i}qn)^{2}}{5}-\frac{(\mathrm{i}pn)(\mathrm{i}qn)^{3}}{15}\Big)\Big({}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{j}\Big)\Big({}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{ij}\Big)\bigg]\,,

(70b)

where the
p=q
case has been excluded from the sum, as it corresponds exactly to the DC piece, which will be treated separately.
It is then immediate to compute its time integral, namely

\displaystyle\Pi_{i}^{s,\text{AC}}

\displaystyle=\frac{G\phi_{0}(3+2\omega_{0})}{c^{5}}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q)\ell}\bigg[\frac{2}{3\phi_{0}}\frac{(\mathrm{i}pn)(\mathrm{i}qn)^{2}}{\mathrm{i}(p+q)n}\Big({}_{p}\widetilde{E}^{s}\Big)\Big({}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{i}\Big)+\frac{2}{15}\frac{(\mathrm{i}pn)^{2}(\mathrm{i}qn)^{3}}{\mathrm{i}(p+q)n}\Big({}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{j}\Big)\Big({}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{ij}\Big)\bigg]\,,

(71a)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i}^{s,\text{AC}}

\displaystyle=\frac{G\phi_{0}(3+2\omega_{0})}{c^{5}}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q)\ell}\bigg[\frac{2}{3\phi_{0}}\bigg(\frac{(\mathrm{i}pn)(\mathrm{i}qn)}{\mathrm{i}(p+q)n}+\frac{(\mathrm{i}pn)(\mathrm{i}qn)^{2}}{\left[\mathrm{i}(p+q)n\right]^{2}}\bigg)\Big({}_{p}\widetilde{E}^{s}\Big)\Big({}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{i}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{5}\bigg(\frac{(\mathrm{i}pn)^{2}(\mathrm{i}qn)^{2}}{\mathrm{i}(p+q)n}-\frac{1}{3}\frac{(\mathrm{i}pn)(\mathrm{i}qn)^{3}}{\mathrm{i}(p+q)n}+\frac{2}{3}\frac{(\mathrm{i}pn)^{2}(\mathrm{i}qn)^{3}}{\left[\mathrm{i}(p+q)n\right]^{2}}\bigg)\Big({}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{j}\Big)\Big({}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{ij}\Big)\bigg]\,,

(71b)

where the
-\infty
bound does not contribute and has been discarded (see
Blanchet and Schäfer 1993
;
Arun et al. 2004
for a justification). Projecting onto the
(x,y,z)
components, leveraging the fact that
\Pi_{i}^{s,\text{AC}}\in\mathbb{R}
, and using the real or purely imaginary character of the Fourier coefficients in Eq. (
29
), I find that
\Gamma_{i}^{s,\text{AC}}=\Gamma_{x}^{s,\text{AC}}n_{0}^{i}+\Gamma_{y}^{s,\text{AC}}\lambda_{0}^{i}
and
\Pi_{i}^{s,\text{AC}}=\Pi_{x}^{s,\text{AC}}n_{0}^{i}+\Pi_{y}^{s,\text{AC}}\lambda_{0}^{i}
, where

\displaystyle\Pi_{x}^{s,\text{AC}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2G\phi_{0}(3+2\omega_{0})}{3c^{5}}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}\cos\Big( (p+q)\ell\Big)\Bigg\{\frac{n^{2}}{\phi_{0}}\,\frac{pq^{2}}{p+q}\,{}_{p}\widetilde{E}^{s}\ {}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}-\frac{n^{4}}{5}\,\frac{p^{2}q^{3}}{p+q}\,\bigg({}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}\ {}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{xx}^{s}+{}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{y}^{s}\ {}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{xy}^{s}\bigg)\Bigg\}\,,

(72a)

\displaystyle\Pi_{y}^{s,\text{AC}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\mathrm{i}G\phi_{0}(3+2\omega_{0})}{3c^{5}}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}\sin\Big( (p+q)\ell\Big)\Bigg\{\frac{n^{2}}{\phi_{0}}\,\frac{pq^{2}}{p+q}\,{}_{p}\widetilde{E}^{s}\ {}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{y}^{s}-\frac{n^{4}}{5}\,\frac{p^{2}q^{3}}{p+q}\,\bigg({}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}\ {}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{xy}^{s}+{}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{y}^{s}\ {}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{yy}^{s}\bigg)\!\Bigg\}\,,

(72b)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{x}^{s,\text{AC}}

\displaystyle=\frac{G\phi_{0}(3+2\omega_{0})}{c^{5}}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}\sin\Big( (p+q)\ell\Big)\Bigg\{-\frac{2n}{3\phi_{0}}\,\frac{pq(p+2q)}{(p+q)^{2}}\,{}_{p}\widetilde{E}^{s}\ {}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{n^{3}}{15}\,\frac{pq^{2}(3p^{2}+4pq-q^{2})}{(p+q)^{2}}\bigg({}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}\ {}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{xx}^{s}+{}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{y}^{s}\ {}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{xy}^{s}\bigg)\Bigg\},

(72c)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{y}^{s,\text{AC}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathrm{i}G\phi_{0}(3+2\omega_{0})}{c^{5}}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}\cos\Big( (p+q)\ell\Big)\Bigg\{-\frac{2n}{3\phi_{0}}\,\frac{pq(p+2q)}{(p+q)^{2}}{}_{p}\widetilde{E}^{s}\ {}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{y}^{s}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\frac{n^{3}}{15}\,\frac{pq^{2}(3p^{2}+4pq-q^{2})}{(p+q)^{2}}\bigg({}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}\ {}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{xy}^{s}+{}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{y}^{s}\ {}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{yy}^{s}\bigg)\Bigg\}\,.

(72d)

Note that the latter contributions vanish upon orbit averaging. Using the QK parametrization (
13
), it is possible to expand the previous expressions in the small eccentricity. These expressions are quite cumbersome, so I will only include the leading correction in the eccentricity. They read:

\displaystyle\Pi_{i}^{s,\text{AC}}

\displaystyle=\frac{16}{15}c\,\zeta m\nu^{2}\mathcal{S}_{-}\,x^{3}\Bigg\{\left(\mathcal{S}_{+}-\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta\right)\Big[\cos(\ell)\,n_{0}^{i}+\sin(\ell)\,\lambda_{0}^{i}\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad+e\Bigg[-\frac{5}{2}\bar{\gamma}^{-1}(\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{+}+\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{-})+\frac{5}{2}\mathcal{S}_{+}-\frac{15}{8}\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta\Bigg]\Bigl(\cos(2\ell)\,n_{0}^{i}+\sin(2\ell)\,\lambda_{0}^{i}\Bigr)+\mathcal{O}(e^{2})\Bigg\}\,,

(73a)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i}^{s,\text{AC}}

\displaystyle=\frac{12}{5c^{2}}\tilde{G}\alpha m^{2}\nu^{2}\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}x^{3/2}\Bigg\{\left(\mathcal{S}_{+}-\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta\right)\Big[\sin(\ell)\,n_{0}^{i}-\cos(\ell)\,\lambda_{0}^{i}\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad+e\Bigg[-\frac{5}{3}\bar{\gamma}^{-1}(\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{+}+\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{-})+\frac{5}{3}\mathcal{S}_{+}-\frac{5}{4}\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta\Bigg]\Bigl(\sin(2\ell)\,n_{0}^{i}-\cos(2\ell)\,\lambda_{0}^{i}\Bigr)+\mathcal{O}(e^{2})\Bigg\}\,.

(73b)

I can then take the limit of circular orbits, re-express everything in terms of the corotating basis
(\bm{n},\bm{\lambda},\bm{\ell})
and then substitute
\bm{\lambda}
and
x
by their expressions in terms of
\bm{v}
and
r
. I then find

\displaystyle\Big[\Pi_{i}^{s,\text{AC}}\Big]_{\text{circ}}

\displaystyle=\frac{16(\tilde{G}\alpha m)^{3}m\nu^{2}\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}(\mathcal{S}_{+}-\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta)}{15c^{5}r^{3}}n^{i}\,,

(74a)

\displaystyle\Big[\Gamma_{i}^{s,\text{AC}}\Big]_{\text{circ}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{12(\tilde{G}\alpha m)m\nu^{2}\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}(\mathcal{S}_{+}-\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta)}{5c^{5}r}v^{i}\,.

(74b)

I have checked that the simpler calculation that assumes circular orbits from the start (and does not use the Fourier decomposition of the multipolar moments) yields the same result, which is an important consistency test of the result and of the Fourier decomposition.

V.3.2
The DC part

Injecting the Keplerian parametrization into Eqs. (
68
), and performing the orbit averaging as usual, I find that the orbit-averaged fluxes of linear momentum and CM position read

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{F}_{s,\bm{G}}^{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{4(\tilde{G}\alpha m)^{3}m\nu^{2}\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}}{15\bar{\gamma}c^{5}}(20\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{+}+20\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{-}-2\bar{\gamma}\mathcal{S}_{+}-3\bar{\gamma}\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta)\,\frac{e}{(1-e^{2})^{3/2}a^{3}}n_{0}^{i}\,,

(75a)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{F}_{s,\bm{P}}^{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{(\tilde{G}\alpha m)^{7/2}m\nu^{2}\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}}{3\bar{\gamma}c^{5}}(4\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{+}+4\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{-}-\bar{\gamma}\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta)\,\frac{e(4+e^{2})}{(1-e^{2})^{3}a^{9/2}}\lambda_{0}^{i}\,.

(75b)

Thanks to the leading-order relations
Trestini 2024

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{\differential e}{\differential t}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{(\tilde{G}\alpha m)^{2}\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\nu}{c^{3}}\times\frac{2e}{a^{3}(1-e^{2})^{3/2}}\,,

(76a)

\displaystyle a(e)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{0}e^{4/3}}{1-e^{2}}\,,

(76b)

one can perform a change of variables in the integrals of (
75
), namely transform an integral over time into an integral over eccentricity. In the far past, the eccentricity asymptotes towards
1
(at leading order in the post-Newtonian expansion), and I found that the associated bound converges in this limit. These integrals can in fact be computed analytically, and I find that
\Pi_{i}^{s,\text{DC}}=\Pi_{y}^{s,\text{DC}}\lambda_{0}^{i}
and
\Gamma_{i}^{s,\text{DC}}=\Gamma_{x}^{s,\text{DC}}n_{0}^{i}+\Gamma_{y}^{s,\text{DC}}\lambda_{0}^{i}
, where

\displaystyle\Pi_{y}^{s,\text{DC}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\tilde{G}\alpha m)^{3/2}m\nu}{6\bar{\gamma}\mathcal{S}_{-}c^{2}}(4\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{+}+4\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{-}-\bar{\gamma}\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta)\frac{e(4+e)}{a^{3/2}(1+e)\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}\,,

(77a)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{x}^{s,\text{DC}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2(\tilde{G}\alpha m)m\nu}{15\bar{\gamma}\mathcal{S}_{-}c^{2}}\Big(20\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{-}+20\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{-}-2\bar{\gamma}\mathcal{S}_{+}-3\bar{\gamma}\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta\Big)(1-e)\,,

(77b)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{y}^{s,\text{DC}}

\displaystyle=\frac{mc}{24(\tilde{G}\alpha m)^{1/2}\bar{\gamma}\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}^{3}}\left(4\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{+}+4\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{-}-\bar{\gamma}\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta\right)\,\frac{a^{3/2}(1-e^{2})}{e^{2}}\Biggl[\!12-\!5e-6e^{2}-\!e^{3}-\!10\sqrt{1-e^{2}}\arctan\sqrt{\frac{1-e}{1+e}}\Biggr].

(77c)

In the
e\rightarrow 0
limit (with
a
kept constant), these functions behave as

\displaystyle\Pi_{y}^{s,\text{DC}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(e)\,,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{x}^{s,\text{DC}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(1)\,,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{y}^{s,\text{DC}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{e^{2}}\right)\,,

(78)

whereas in the
e\rightarrow 1
limit, they behave as

\displaystyle\Pi_{y}^{s,\text{DC}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-e}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{x}^{s,\text{DC}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(1-e)\,,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{y}^{s,\text{DC}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left((1-e)^{2}\right)\,.

(79)

One can interpret these quantities as the boost and shift associated to the secular piece of the gravitational recoil. The boost term
\bm{\Pi}^{s,\text{DC}}
vanishes in the limit of vanishing
current
eccentricity, which smoothly connects with the predictions that, for an eternally quasicircular orbit, there is no secular boost, at least in the post-Newtonian, adiabatic regime. However, the
y
-component of the shift term
\bm{\Gamma}^{s,\text{DC}}
blows up as the current eccentricity goes to zero, whilst the
x
-component goes to a nonzero finite limit. This behavior appears to be paradoxical, since in the quasicircular limit, the shift is predicted to be strictly zero. This paradox is also observed in the case of general relativity
Trestini
, although with more complicated analytical expressions. Since only
\bm{\Pi}^{s,\text{DC}}
will be required in this work, the complete resolution of this paradox is left to future work. However, I will state a few mitigating arguments. First, assuming quasicircular orbits artificially imposes a symmetry which does not exist in the general case, because an ellipse exhibits a preferred direction, but not a circle. Moreover, the quasicircular model implies that the eccentricity was zero in the infinite past — whereas assuming that the current eccentricity is nonzero but arbitrarily small implies that the eccentricity was one in the infinite past (up to small PN corrections due to the radiation). Thus, it is not entirely surprising that there could be a nonsmooth limit when going from the quasielliptic to the quasicircular scenario, although a smooth limit would of course more satisfactory. Second, in most cases, only the center-of-inertia is physically relevant, and a constant shift between two frames is unlikely to affect any physical observable. Thus, it should be possible to renormalize this apparent divergence by a physically irrelevant, constant shift. Instead of working in the center-of-mass frame, defined uniquely by
G_{i}+\Gamma_{i}^{s}+\Gamma_{i}=0
, one would then work in a center-of-inertia frame, defined up to an arbitrary constant shift by
P_{i}+\Pi_{i}^{s}+\Pi_{i}=0
.

V.4
The scalar dipolar moment as 2.5PN in the CM frame

When computing the 1.5PN energy flux in the CM frame
Bernard et al. 2022
, the scalar dipole was obtained at 2.5PN order in a generic frame. But only
\partial_{t}^{2}{\mathrm{I}}{}_{i}^{s}
was needed with 2.5PN accuracy in the CM frame, and not
{\mathrm{I}}{}_{i}^{s}
, so only the 1.5PN-accurate formula for the passage to the CM was required (see App. B1 of
35
for an explanation). However, the angular momentum flux I will be computing hereafter involves
\partial_{t}{\mathrm{I}}{}_{i}^{s}
, so the 2.5PN-accurate formula for the passage to the CM is necessary.
7
7

7

A contrario
, in GR, the quadrupolar moment
\mathrm{I}_{ij}
has a particular structure that avoids the appearance of this nonlocal term at 3.5PN order, see
Blanchet et al. 2024
.
The nonlocal term (
55
) will thus propagate into the expression of the scalar dipole moment in the CM frame, which is thus split as follows:

{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{i}={\mathrm{I}}^{s,\text{inst}}_{i}+\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}\zeta(\mathcal{S}_{+}+\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta)}{(1-\zeta)\phi_{0}}\Gamma_{i}^{s}\,,

(80)

where
{\mathrm{I}}^{s,\text{inst}}_{i}
is instantaneous and relegated to App.
F
, and
\Gamma_{i}^{s}
is defined in (
55
). However, the angular momentum flux does not depend directly on
\mathrm{I}^{s}_{i}
, but rather on its time derivative
\partial_{t}{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{i}
, which is obtained thanks to the identity
\differential\Gamma^{s}_{i}/\differential t=\Pi^{s}_{i}+\mathcal{F}^{i}_{s,\bm{G}}
, where
\Pi_{i}^{s}
is defined in (
56
) and
\mathcal{F}^{i}_{s,\bm{G}}
is explicitly presented in (
68
). The first derivative of the scalar dipole is then split as follows:

\overset{(1)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{i}=\overset{(1)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s,\text{inst}}_{i}+\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}\zeta(\mathcal{S}_{+}+\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta)}{(1-\zeta)\phi_{0}}\Big(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{s,\bm{G}}+\Pi_{i}^{s}\Big)\,.

(81)

Finally, using the identity
\differential\Pi^{s}_{i}/\differential t=\mathcal{F}^{i}_{s,\bm{P}}
, I find that

\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{i}=\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s,\text{inst}}_{i}+\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}\zeta(\mathcal{S}_{+}+\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta)}{(1-\zeta)\phi_{0}}\Big(\overset{(1)}{\mathcal{F}}{}^{i}_{s,\bm{G}}+\mathcal{F}^{i}_{s,\bm{P}}\Big)\,,

(82)

which is purely instantaneous. Importantly, I have checked that this result is in agreement with the corresponding expression used in
35
, which was derived without the knowledge of the 2.5PN-accurate formula for the passage to the CM frame.

VI
The flux of energy and angular momentum

This section is devoted to the derivation of the orbit-averaged fluxes of energy and angular momentum sourced by quasielliptic binary systems. These quantities are very important as they are required for the derivation of the secular evolution of the orbital elements. They were obtained at Newtonian order in
47
(i.e. at relative 1PN order beyond to the leading
-
1PN order dipolar fluxes), and I will now extend this result to 1.5PN order, which is the same order at which the energy flux generated by
circular orbits
was derived in
35
. Due to the presence of hereditary tail and memory integrals, the difficulty of the computation is comparable to the 2.5PN flux in general relativity
Arun et al. 2008a
;
Arun et al. 2008b
;
Arun et al. 2009
.

VI.1
General expressions in terms of the source moments

Recall that the expressions of the fluxes in terms of the radiative moments read
Thorne 1980
;
Blanchet 2014
;
Compère et al. 2020
;
Bernard et al. 2022
;
Trestini 2024

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}\frac{G\phi_{0}}{c^{2\ell+1}}\Big(\alpha_{\ell}\,\overset{(1)}{\mathcal{U}}_{L}\overset{(1)}{\mathcal{U}}_{L}+\frac{\beta_{\ell}}{c^{2}}\overset{(1)}{\mathcal{V}}_{L}\overset{(1)}{\mathcal{V}}_{L}\Big)\,,

(83a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{s}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{G\phi_{0}(3+2\omega_{0})}{c^{2\ell+1}}\gamma_{\ell}\overset{(1)}{\mathcal{U}}{}^{s}_{L}\overset{(1)}{\mathcal{U}}{}^{s}_{L}\,,

(83b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{i}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{ijk}\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}\frac{G\phi_{0}}{c^{2\ell+1}}\Big(\alpha^{\prime}_{\ell}\,\mathcal{U}_{jL-1}\overset{(1)}{\mathcal{U}}_{kL-1}+\frac{\beta^{\prime}_{\ell}}{c^{2}}\mathcal{V}_{jL-1}\overset{(1)}{\mathcal{V}}_{kL-1}\Big)\,,

(83c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{s}_{i}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{ijk}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\frac{G\phi_{0}(3+2\omega_{0})}{c^{2\ell+1}}\gamma^{\prime}_{\ell}\,\mathcal{U}^{s}_{jL-1}\overset{(1)}{\mathcal{U}}{}^{s}_{kL-1}\,.

(83d)

where

\displaystyle\alpha_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\ell+1)(\ell+2)}{(\ell-1)\ell\ell!(2\ell+1)!!}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\beta_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\ell(\ell+2)}{(\ell-1)(\ell+1)!(2\ell+1)!!}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\gamma_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\ell!(2\ell+1)!!}\,,

(84)

and
\alpha^{\prime}_{\ell}=\ell\,\alpha_{\ell}
,
\beta^{\prime}_{\ell}=\ell\,\beta_{\ell}
, and
\gamma^{\prime}_{\ell}=\ell\,\gamma_{\ell}
.

Anticipating the fact that nonspinning binaries do not undergo precession, I will define
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}_{i}\ell_{i}
and
\mathcal{G}^{s}=\mathcal{G}^{s}_{i}\ell_{i}
. Indeed, the angular momentum vector will always be along
\ell_{i}
, which is a constant vector of norm
1
, and always orthogonal to the orbital plane.
Replacing the radiative moments by their expressions in terms of the source moments [given by (3.28-29) of
Bernard et al. 2022
], I find that, at 1.5PN order beyond quadrupolar radiation, the fluxes can be divided in different pieces: instantaneous, tails, memory, and the new nonlocal
\bm{\Pi}^{s}
contribution. This decomposition reads:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{inst}}+\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{tail}}\,,

(85a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{s}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{s,\mathrm{inst}}+\mathcal{F}^{s,\mathrm{tail}}\,,

(85b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{G}_{i}^{\mathrm{inst}}+\mathcal{G}_{i}^{\mathrm{tail}}+\mathcal{G}_{i}^{\mathrm{mem}}\,,

(85c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{s}_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{G}_{i}^{s,\mathrm{inst}}+\mathcal{G}_{i}^{s,\bm{\Pi}^{s}}+\mathcal{G}_{i}^{s,\mathrm{tail}}\,.

(85d)

The memory term, present only in the tensor angular momentum flux, reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{i}^{\mathrm{mem}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2G^{2}\phi_{0}(3+2\omega_{0})}{15c^{8}}\epsilon_{ik(j}\!\!\overset{(3)}{\mathrm{I}}\!{}_{a)k}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\!\differential\tau\big[\!\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}\!{}^{s}_{a}\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}\!{}^{s}_{j}\big](u-\tau)\,,

(86)

which is in agreement with (4.11) of
Jain and Rettegno 2024
.

The tails terms read:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{tail}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4G^{2}\mathrm{M}}{5c^{8}}\overset{(3)}{\mathrm{I}}\!{}_{ij}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\overset{(5)}{\mathrm{I}}\!{}_{ij}(u-\tau)\left[\ln\left(\frac{c\tau}{2b_{0}}\right)+\frac{11}{12}\right]\,,

(87a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{s,\mathrm{tail}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4G^{2}\mathrm{M}(3+2\omega_{0})}{c^{6}}\Bigg\{\frac{1}{3}\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}\!{}^{s}_{i}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\overset{(4)}{\mathrm{I}}\!{}^{s}_{i}(u-\tau)\left[\ln\left(\frac{c\tau}{2b_{0}}\right)+1\right]+\frac{\overset{(1)}{E}{}^{s}}{c^{2}\phi_{0}^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\overset{(3)}{E}{}^{s}(u-\tau)\ln\left(\frac{c\tau}{2b_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{30c^{2}}\overset{(3)}{\mathrm{I}}\!{}^{s}_{ij}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,\overset{(5)}{\mathrm{I}}\!{}^{s}_{ij}(u-\tau)\left[\ln\left(\frac{c\tau}{2b_{0}}\right)+\frac{3}{2}\right]\Bigg\}\,,

(87b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{i}^{\mathrm{tail}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4G^{2}\mathrm{M}}{5c^{8}}\epsilon_{ijk}\Bigg\{\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}\!{}_{ja}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\overset{(5)}{\mathrm{I}}\!{}_{ka}(u-\tau)\left[\ln\left(\frac{c\tau}{2b_{0}}\right)+\frac{11}{12}\right]-\overset{(3)}{\mathrm{I}}\!{}_{ja}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\overset{(4)}{\mathrm{I}}\!{}_{ka}(u-\tau)\left[\ln\left(\frac{c\tau}{2b_{0}}\right)+\frac{11}{12}\right]\Bigg\}\,,

(87c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{i}^{s,\mathrm{tail}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2G^{2}\mathrm{M}(3+2\omega_{0})}{3c^{6}}\epsilon_{ijk}\Bigg\{\overset{(1)}{\mathrm{I}}\!{}^{s}_{j}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\overset{(4)}{\mathrm{I}}\!{}^{s}_{k}(u-\tau)\left[\ln\left(\frac{c\tau}{2b_{0}}\right)+1\right]-\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}\!{}^{s}_{j}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\overset{(3)}{\mathrm{I}}\!{}^{s}_{k}(u-\tau)\left[\ln\left(\frac{c\tau}{2b_{0}}\right)+1\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad+\frac{1}{5c^{2}}\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}\!{}^{s}_{ja}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,\overset{(5)}{\mathrm{I}}\!{}^{s}_{ka}(u-\tau)\left[\ln\left(\frac{c\tau}{2b_{0}}\right)+\frac{3}{2}\right]-\frac{1}{5c^{2}}\overset{(3)}{\mathrm{I}}\!{}^{s}_{ja}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,\overset{(4)}{\mathrm{I}}\!{}^{s}_{ka}(u-\tau)\left[\ln\left(\frac{c\tau}{2b_{0}}\right)+\frac{3}{2}\right]\Bigg\}\,,

(87d)

which is also in agreement with (4.10) and (5.17) of
Jain and Rettegno 2024
.

The nonlocal
\bm{\Pi}^{s}
contribution to the angular momentum flux arises from the nonlocal contribution in the passage to the CM, see Sec.
V
. Note that in the energy flux, both dipolar moments entering at Newtonian order bear two time derivatives, which has the effect of “localizing” the
\bm{\Pi}^{s}
contribution, see (
82
). However, the angular momentum flux contains a contribution (entering at Newtonian order) from the first time derivative of the dipolar moment, which will thus feature
\bm{\Pi}^{s}
. This contribution reads:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{i}^{s,\bm{\Pi}^{s}}

\displaystyle=\frac{G(3+2\omega_{0})}{3c^{3}}\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}\zeta(\mathcal{S}_{+}+\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta)}{(1-\zeta)}\epsilon_{ijk}\,\Pi_{j}^{s}\!\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}_{k}^{s}

(88)

Finally, the instantaneous fluxes are defined as what remains, namely
