\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{\omega_{0}^{\prime}\phi_{0}}{3+2\omega_{0}}\Bigg(20\frac{\overset{(1)}{E}{\!}^{s}}{\phi_{0}}\overset{(1)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{j}\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{k}+10\frac{\overset{(2)}{E}{\!}^{s}}{\phi_{0}}\mathrm{I}^{s}_{j}\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{k}-10\frac{\overset{(3)}{E}{\!}^{s}}{\phi_{0}}\mathrm{I}^{s}_{j}\overset{(1)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{k}+2\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{j}\overset{(3)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{a}\overset{(1)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{ka}-2\overset{(1)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{j}\overset{(3)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{a}\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{ka}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad-4\overset{(1)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{j}\overset{(4)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{a}\overset{(1)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{ka}+2\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{j}\overset{(4)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{a}\mathrm{I}^{s}_{ka}-2\overset{(1)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{j}\overset{(5)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{a}\mathrm{I}^{s}_{ka}\Bigg)\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+5\mathrm{J}_{i}\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{a}\overset{(3)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{a}-5\mathrm{J}_{a}\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{a}\overset{(3)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{i}-5\mathrm{J}_{i}\overset{(1)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{a}\overset{(4)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{a}+5\mathrm{J}_{a}\overset{(1)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{a}\overset{(4)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}^{s}_{i}\Bigg]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{9}}\right)\,.

(89d)

VI.2
The instananeous fluxes

The instantaneous fluxes are first computed at 1.5PN order in terms of the phase space variables
(\bm{y}_{1},\bm{y}_{2},\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2})
by (i) replacing the source source multipolar moments by their expressions in the CM frame, and (ii) computing the time derivatives using the equations of motion in the CM frame. The required source moments are almost all provided in App. B1 of
35
, except for the scalar dipole moment
\mathrm{I}^{s}_{i}
, which is presented at 2PN order instead of 2.5PN (see the discussion therein). The full scalar dipole at 2.5PN order is presented for the first time in App.
F
, and it features a nonlocal piece arising from the passage to the CM frame. I also provide all the source moments in machine-readable form in the Supplemental Material
Sup
. The equations of motion (both in a general frame and in the CM frame) are given at required order in
Mirshekari and Will 2013
, and I have provided these in a machine-readable form in the Supplemental Material
Sup
as well.

The expressions of the energy fluxes in terms of the orbital variables in the CM frame, namely
\bm{x}=r\bm{n}
and
\bm{v}
, were already provided at 1.5PN order in (4.10) and (C2) of
35
, confirming the previously known 1PN result in the tensor sector [(6.5) of
Lang 2015
] and correcting the 1PN result in the scalar sector [(6.7-8) of
Lang 2015
]. Here, I provide for the angular momentum fluxes at 1.5PN in terms of the orbital variables in the CM frame:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{i}^{\mathrm{inst}}

\displaystyle=\frac{16(1-\zeta)m\nu^{2}}{5\alpha c^{5}}\Big(\frac{\tilde{G}\alpha m}{r}\Big)^{2}\epsilon_{ijk}n_{j}v_{k}\Bigg\{\frac{\tilde{G}\alpha m}{r}-\frac{3}{2}(nv)^{2}+v^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{c^{2}}\Bigg[\Big(-\frac{745}{84}-4\bar{\beta}_{+}-\frac{7}{2}\bar{\gamma}+4\bar{\beta}_{-}\delta+\frac{1}{42}\nu\Big)\Big(\frac{\tilde{G}\alpha m}{r}\Big)^{2}+\Big(\frac{31}{7}+4\bar{\beta}_{+}+\bar{\gamma}-4\bar{\beta}_{-}\delta+\frac{197}{84}\nu\Big)\Big(\frac{\tilde{G}\alpha m}{r}\Big)(nv)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\Big(\frac{95}{56}-\frac{45}{7}\nu\Big)(nv)^{4}+\Big(-\frac{29}{21}-2\bar{\beta}_{+}+2\bar{\beta}_{-}\delta-\frac{95}{42}\nu\Big)\Big(\frac{\tilde{G}\alpha m}{r}\Big)v^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\Big(-\frac{37}{14}+\frac{277}{28}\nu\Big)(nv)^{2}v^{2}+\Big(\frac{307}{168}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{\gamma}-\frac{137}{42}\nu\Big)v^{4}\Bigg]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{c^{3}}\Bigg[\Big(\frac{\tilde{G}\alpha m}{r}\Big)^{2}\Big(-\frac{1}{4}\bar{\gamma}-\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\nu\Big)(nv)+\frac{5}{8}\bar{\gamma}\Big(\frac{\tilde{G}\alpha m}{r}\Big)(nv)^{3}-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\gamma}\Big(\frac{\tilde{G}\alpha m}{r}\Big)(nv)v^{2}\Bigg]\Bigg\}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{9}}\right)\,,

(90a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{i}^{s,\mathrm{inst}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m\nu^{2}}{3c^{3}}\Big(\frac{\tilde{G}\alpha m}{r}\Big)^{2}\epsilon_{ijk}n_{j}v_{k}\Bigg\{\widetilde{A}^{-1\text{PN}}+\frac{1}{c^{2}}\Bigg[\widetilde{B}_{1}^{\text{N}}\Big(\frac{\tilde{G}\alpha m}{r}\Big)+\widetilde{B}_{2}^{\text{N}}(nv)^{2}+\widetilde{B}_{3}^{\text{N}}v^{2}\Bigg]+\frac{1}{c^{3}}\widetilde{C}^{0.5\text{PN}}\Big(\frac{\tilde{G}\alpha m}{r}\Big)(nv)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{c^{4}}\Bigg[\widetilde{D}_{1}^{1\text{PN}}\Big(\frac{\tilde{G}\alpha m}{r}\Big)^{2}+\widetilde{D}_{2}^{1\text{PN}}\Big(\frac{\tilde{G}\alpha m}{r}\Big)(nv)^{2}+\widetilde{D}_{3}^{1\text{PN}}\Big(\frac{\tilde{G}\alpha m}{r}\Big)v^{2}+\widetilde{D}_{4}^{1\text{PN}}(nv)^{4}+\widetilde{D}_{5}^{1\text{PN}}(nv)^{2}v^{2}+\widetilde{D}_{6}^{1\text{PN}}v^{4}\Bigg]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{c^{5}}\Bigg[\widetilde{E}_{1}^{1.5\text{PN}}\Big(\frac{\tilde{G}\alpha m}{r}\Big)^{2}(nv)+\widetilde{E}_{2}^{1.5\text{PN}}\Big(\frac{\tilde{G}\alpha m}{r}\Big)(nv)^{3}+\widetilde{E}_{3}^{1.5\text{PN}}\Big(\frac{\tilde{G}\alpha m}{r}\Big)(nv)v^{2}\Bigg]\Bigg\}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{9}}\right)\,,

(90b)

where the coefficients associated to (
90
) are given in App.
G
.
The tensor flux (
90
) is in full agreement with (4.7-8) of
Jain and Rettegno 2024
at 1.5PN, and the scalar flux (
90
) is in full agreement with (5.00-15) of
Jain and Rettegno 2024
at 1PN. The 1.5PN piece of
\mathcal{G}^{s,\mathrm{inst}}_{i}
was computed for the first time in this work. All these results can also be found in machine-readable form in the Supplemental Material
Sup
.

Using the identities
(nv)=\dot{r}
,
v^{2}=\dot{r}^{2}+r^{2}\dot{\phi}^{2}
, and
\epsilon_{ijk}n_{j}v_{k}=r\dot{\phi}\ell^{i}
, I then obtain the fluxes in terms of
(r,\phi,\dot{r},\dot{\phi})
. Specializing to the case of quasielliptic orbits, I can then use the identities
\dot{r}=\mathcal{R}(r^{-1},E,J)
and
\dot{\phi}=\mathcal{S}(r^{-1},E,J)
, given in (3.1) of
47
, along with the quasi-Keplerian representation (
13
), to obtain the fluxes only in terms of the eccentric anomaly
u
and the QK parameters.
When computing
\left\langle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{inst}}\right\rangle
,
\left\langle\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{inst}}\right\rangle
,
\left\langle\mathcal{F}^{s,\mathrm{inst}}\right\rangle
and
\left\langle\mathcal{G}^{s,\mathrm{inst}}\right\rangle
, all the QK parameters are first reexpressed in terms only of
x
and
e_{t}
[using (C1) of
47
], and one can then always use trigonometric relations to write the orbit averaged fluxes as a linear combinations of the following master integrals,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\differential u}{(1-e_{t}\cos u)^{n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1-e_{t}^{2})^{n/2}}P_{n-1}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-e_{t}^{2}}}\right)\,,

(91a)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\differential u\,\sin u}{(1-e_{t}\cos u)^{n}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(91b)

where
n\geq 1
and
P_{n}(x)
is the
n
-th Legendre polynomial. Finally, I find that the instantaneous fluxes read, for the tensor sector,

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{F}^{\text{inst}}\right\rangle=\frac{32c^{5}(1+\bar{\gamma}/2)x^{5}\nu^{2}}{5\tilde{G}\alpha}\Bigg\{\frac{1+\tfrac{73}{24}e_{t}^{2}+\tfrac{37}{96}e_{t}^{4}}{(1-e_{t}^{2})^{7/2}}

\displaystyle\ \ \qquad+\frac{x}{(1-e_{t}^{2})^{9/2}}\Bigg[-\tfrac{1247}{336}-\tfrac{8}{3}\bar{\beta}_{+}-\tfrac{4}{3}\bar{\gamma}+\tfrac{8}{3}\bar{\beta}_{-}\delta-\tfrac{35}{12}\nu+e_{t}^{2}\bigg(\tfrac{10475}{672}-\tfrac{571}{36}\bar{\beta}_{+}+\tfrac{379}{36}\bar{\gamma}+\tfrac{571}{36}\bar{\beta}_{-}\delta-\tfrac{1081}{36}\nu\bigg)

\displaystyle\ \ \quad\qquad+e_{t}^{4}\bigg(\tfrac{10043}{384}-\tfrac{1003}{144}\bar{\beta}_{+}+\tfrac{955}{72}\bar{\gamma}+\tfrac{1003}{144}\bar{\beta}_{-}\delta-\tfrac{311}{12}\nu\bigg)+e_{t}^{6}\bigg(\tfrac{2179}{1792}+\tfrac{131}{288}\bar{\gamma}-\tfrac{851}{576}\nu\bigg)\Bigg]+\mathcal{O}(x^{2})\Bigg\}\,,

(92a)

\displaystyle\ \ \quad\left\langle\mathcal{G}^{\text{inst}}\right\rangle=\frac{32c^{2}(1+\bar{\gamma}/2)m\nu^{2}x^{7/2}}{5}\Bigg\{\frac{1+\tfrac{7}{8}e_{t}^{2}}{(1-e_{t}^{2})^{2}}

\displaystyle\ \qquad\quad+\frac{x}{(1-e_{t}^{2})^{3}}\Bigg[-\tfrac{1247}{336}-\tfrac{8}{3}\bar{\beta}_{+}-\tfrac{4}{3}\bar{\gamma}+\tfrac{8}{3}\bar{\beta}_{-}\delta-\tfrac{35}{12}\nu+e_{t}^{2}\bigg(\tfrac{3019}{336}-\tfrac{35}{6}\bar{\beta}_{+}+\tfrac{21}{4}\bar{\gamma}+\tfrac{35}{6}\bar{\beta}_{-}\delta-\tfrac{335}{24}\nu\bigg)

\displaystyle\ \ \quad\qquad\qquad+e_{t}^{4}\bigg(\tfrac{8399}{2688}-\tfrac{1}{4}\bar{\beta}_{+}+\tfrac{67}{48}\bar{\gamma}+\tfrac{1}{4}\bar{\beta}_{-}\delta-\tfrac{275}{96}\nu\bigg)\Bigg]+\mathcal{O}(x^{2})\Bigg\}\,,

(92b)

and are given in the scalar scalar by

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{F}^{s,\mathrm{inst}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{c^{5}\nu^{2}\zeta x^{5}}{3\tilde{G}\alpha}\Bigg(4\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}x^{-1}\frac{1+\frac{1}{2}e_{t}^{2}}{(1-e_{t}^{2})^{5/2}}+\frac{\mathfrak{f}_{1}+\mathfrak{f}_{2}e_{t}^{2}+\mathfrak{f}_{3}e_{t}^{4}}{(1-e_{t}^{2})^{7/2}}+x\Bigg[\frac{\mathfrak{f}_{4}+\mathfrak{f}_{5}e_{t}^{2}+\mathfrak{f}_{6}e_{t}^{4}+\mathfrak{f}_{7}e_{t}^{6}}{(1-e_{t}^{2})^{9/2}}+\frac{\mathfrak{f}_{8}+\mathfrak{f}_{9}e_{t}^{2}+\mathfrak{f}_{10}e_{t}^{4}}{(1-e_{t}^{2})^{4}}\Bigg]+\mathcal{O}(x^{2})\Bigg)\,,

(93a)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{G}^{s,\mathrm{inst}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{c^{2}m\nu^{2}\zeta x^{7/2}}{3}\Bigg(\frac{4\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}x^{-1}}{1-e_{t}^{2}}+\frac{\mathfrak{g}^{s}_{1}+\mathfrak{g}^{s}_{2}e_{t}^{2}}{(1-e_{t}^{2})^{2}}+x\Bigg[\frac{\mathfrak{g}_{3}+\mathfrak{g}_{4}e_{t}^{2}+\mathfrak{g}_{5}e_{t}^{4}}{(1-e_{t}^{2})^{3}}+\frac{\mathfrak{g}_{6}+\mathfrak{g}_{6}e_{t}^{2}+\mathfrak{g}_{7}e_{t}^{4}}{(1-e_{t}^{2})^{7/2}}\Bigg]+\mathcal{O}(x^{2})\Bigg)\,,

(93b)

where the expressions of the coefficients
(\mathfrak{f}_{n},\mathfrak{g}_{n})
are relegated to App.
D
. These fluxes are also presented in machine-readable form the Supplemental Material
Sup
.

VI.3
The
\mathbf{\Pi}^{s}
flux

As we know from Sec.
V.3
, one can decompose
\Pi^{s}_{i}
into an AC and a DC contribution. We propagate this decomposition to the flux, namely

\mathcal{G}_{i}^{s,\bm{\Pi}^{s}}=\mathcal{G}_{i}^{s,\bm{\Pi}^{s}_{\text{AC}}}+\mathcal{G}_{i}^{s,\bm{\Pi}^{s}_{\text{DC}}}
where

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{i}^{s,\bm{\Pi}^{s}_{\text{AC}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{G(3+2\omega_{0})}{3c^{3}}\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}\zeta(\mathcal{S}_{+}+\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta)}{(1-\zeta)}\epsilon_{ijk}\,\Pi_{j}^{s,\text{AC}}\,\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}_{k}^{s}\,,

(94a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{i}^{s,\bm{\Pi}^{s}_{\text{DC}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{G(3+2\omega_{0})}{3c^{3}}\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}\zeta(\mathcal{S}_{+}+\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta)}{(1-\zeta)}\epsilon_{ijk}\,\Pi_{j}^{s,\text{DC}}\,\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}_{k}^{s}\,.

(94b)

The orbit averaged DC piece of the flux vanishes, because

\displaystyle\Big\langle\mathcal{G}_{i}^{s,\bm{\Pi}^{s}_{\text{DC}}}\Big\rangle

\displaystyle\propto\ \epsilon_{ijk}\,\Pi_{j}^{s,\text{DC}}\,\Big\langle\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}_{k}^{s}\,\Big\rangle=0

(95)

where I have use the fact that
\bm{\Pi}^{s}_{\text{DC}}
is approximately constant over an orbit and that time derivatives of multipolar moments vanish upon orbit averaging. Morover, even before orbit averaging, this contribution is finite and vanishes for
e\rightarrow 0
.

Conversely, the AC flux is best expressed by replacing the multipolar moments by their expression as a Fourier series (see Sec.
IV
). After performing explicitly the time integral in
\Pi_{j}^{s,\text{AC}}
, I explicitly find

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{i}^{s,\bm{\Pi}^{s}_{\text{AC}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2G^{2}\phi_{0}(3+2\omega_{0})^{2}\sqrt{\alpha}\zeta(\mathcal{S}_{+}+\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta)}{9(1-\zeta)c^{8}}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\epsilon_{ijk}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q+r)\ell}\frac{(\mathrm{i}rn)^{2}}{\mathrm{i}(p+q)n}\Big({}_{r}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{k}\Big)\bigg[\frac{(\mathrm{i}pn)(\mathrm{i}qn)^{2}}{\phi_{0}}\Big({}_{p}\widetilde{E}^{s}\Big)\Big({}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{j}\Big)+\frac{(\mathrm{i}pn)^{2}(\mathrm{i}qn)^{3}}{5}\Big({}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{a}\Big)\Big({}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{ja}\Big)\bigg].

(96)

Orbit averaging the previous equation is quite simple thanks to the identity
\langle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q+r)\ell}\rangle=\delta_{p+q+r}^{0}
, where
\delta^{a}_{b}
is the Kronecker symbol. I then obtain

\displaystyle\Big\langle\ \mathcal{G}_{i}^{s,\bm{\Pi}^{s}_{\text{AC}}}\Big\rangle\

\displaystyle=\frac{2G^{2}\phi_{0}(3+2\omega_{0})^{2}\sqrt{\alpha}\zeta(\mathcal{S}_{+}+\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta)}{9(1-\zeta)c^{8}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\epsilon_{ijk}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}\bigg[\frac{n^{4}pq^{2}(p+q)}{\phi_{0}}\Big({}_{p}\widetilde{E}^{s}\Big)\Big({}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{j}\Big)\Big({}_{-p-q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{k}\Big)-\frac{n^{6}p^{2}q^{3}(p+q)}{5}\Big({}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{a}\Big)\Big({}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{ja}\Big)\Big({}_{-p-q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{k}\Big)\bigg].

(97)

Since the motion is planar, only the
z
component of the angular momentum flux is nonvanishing, and a simple analysis involving the explicit expressions of the Fourier coefficients [given by Eqs. (
29
)] leads to the conclusion that
\big\langle\ \mathcal{G}_{z}^{s,\bm{\Pi}^{s}_{\text{AC}}}\big\rangle\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
. But of course, it is also true that
\big\langle\ \mathcal{G}_{z}^{s,\bm{\Pi}^{s}_{\text{AC}}}\big\rangle\in\mathbb{R}
, which implies that

\Big\langle\ \mathcal{G}_{i}^{s,\bm{\Pi}^{s}_{\text{AC}}}\Big\rangle\ =0\,.

(98)

In summary, I have found that the
\bm{\Pi}^{s}
contribution to the angular momentum flux entire vanishes after orbit averaging, namely

\Big\langle\ \mathcal{G}_{i}^{s,\bm{\Pi}^{s}}\Big\rangle\ =0\,.

(99)

VI.4
The tail fluxes

The tail fluxes, given by (
VI.1
), feature the difficulty of being nonlocal in time with a logarithmic kernel. These are best treated
Blanchet and Schäfer 1993
;
Arun et al. 2008a
;
Loutrel and Yunes 2017
by replacing all multipolar moments by their Fourier decomposition, as given in Sec.
IV
. Once this replacement is performed, the nonlocal integrals can all be performed using the master formula
Arun et al. 2008a
;
Loutrel and Yunes 2017

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}\differential\tau\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+rk)n\tau}\ln\left(\frac{c\tau}{2b_{0}}\right)=-\frac{1}{pn}\left(1-\frac{rk}{p}\right)\left[\frac{\pi}{2}\mathrm{sg}(p)+\mathrm{i}\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}+\mathrm{i}\ln\left(\frac{2|p|nb_{0}}{c}\right)\right]-\frac{\mathrm{i}rk}{p^{2}n}+\mathcal{O}(k^{2})\,,

(100)

which simplifies straightforwardly at Newtonian order (i.e. in the
k\rightarrow 0
limit). Note that the case
p=0
never occurs, because the multipolar moments in the nonlocal integrals always bear enough time derivatives, which is equivalent to multiplying the Fourier coefficients by some power of
\mathrm{i}np
. The flux can be written as a sum over the relative integers
p
,
q
,
r
and
s
, where each term is proportional to
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\ell[(r+s)k+p+q]}
[
r+s=0
is allowed]. It is then straightforward to perform the time averaging thanks to the formula
Arun et al. 2008a
;
Loutrel and Yunes 2017

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\ell(p+rk)}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\Bigg\{\begin{matrix}1+\mathrm{i}\pi rk\qquad\text{if $p=0$}\\
\frac{r}{p}k\qquad\qquad\,\ \text{if $p\neq 0$}\end{matrix}\Bigg\}+\mathcal{O}(k^{2})\,.

(101)

After some manipulating some sums, I find as expected that the flux is real-valued, and that all arbitrary constants
b_{0}
vanish from the final result, which read

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{tail}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{4\pi G^{2}\mathrm{M}n^{7}}{5c^{8}}\left(\mathcal{I}_{2}\right)^{2}\rho_{2}(e_{t})\,,

(102a)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{G}_{i}^{\mathrm{tail}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{8\mathrm{i}\pi G^{2}\mathrm{M}n^{6}}{5c^{8}}\left(\mathcal{I}_{2}\right)^{2}\tilde{\rho}_{2}(e_{t})\,,

(102b)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{F}^{s,\mathrm{tail}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{4\pi G^{2}\mathrm{M}(3+2\omega_{0})}{c^{6}}\Bigg\{\frac{n^{5}}{3}\left(\mathcal{I}^{s}_{1}\right)^{2}\left[\rho^{s}_{1}(e_{t})+5k\sigma^{s}_{1}(e_{t})\right]+\frac{n^{7}}{30c^{2}}\left(\mathcal{I}^{s}_{2}\right)^{2}\rho_{2}^{s}(e_{t})+\frac{n^{3}}{\phi_{0}^{2}c^{2}}\left(\mathcal{E}^{s}_{0}\right)^{2}\rho_{0}^{s}(e_{t})\Bigg\}\,,

(102c)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{G}_{i}^{s,\mathrm{tail}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{4\mathrm{i}\pi G^{2}\mathrm{M}(3+2\omega_{0})}{c^{6}}\Bigg\{\frac{n^{4}}{3}\left(\mathcal{I}^{s}_{1}\right)^{2}\left[\tilde{\rho}_{1}^{s}(e_{t})+4k\tilde{\sigma}_{1}^{s}(e_{t})\right]+\frac{n^{6}}{15c^{2}}\left(\mathcal{I}^{s}_{2}\right)^{2}\tilde{\rho}_{2}^{s}(e_{t})\Bigg\}\,,

(102d)

where I have introduced in this intermediate step some
unnormalized enhancement functions
, defined by

\displaystyle\rho_{2}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{7}\left({}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}\right)\left({}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}\right)^{\!*}\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{2}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{ijk}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{6}\left({}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ja}\right)\left({}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ka}\right)^{\!*}\,,

\displaystyle\rho_{2}^{s}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{7}\left({}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{ij}\right)\left({}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{ij}\right)^{\!*}\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}^{s}_{2}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{ijk}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{6}\left({}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{ja}\right)\left({}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{ka}\right)^{\!*}\,,

\displaystyle\rho_{1}^{s}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{5}\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\{-1,1\}\\
s\in\{-1,1\}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{i}\right)\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{i}\right)^{*}\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{1}^{s}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{ijk}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{4}\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\{-1,1\}\\
s\in\{-1,1\}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{j}\right)\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{k}\right)^{*}\,,

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}^{s}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{4}\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{m\in\{-1,1\}}\!\!\!\!\!\!m\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{i}\right)\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{i}\right)^{*}\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\sigma}_{1}^{s}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{ijk}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{3}\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{m\in\{-1,1\}}\!\!\!\!\!\!m\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{j}\right)\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s}_{k}\right)^{*}\,,

\displaystyle\rho_{0}^{s}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{3}\left({}_{p}\widehat{E}^{s}\right)\left({}_{p}\widehat{E}^{s}\right)^{\!*}\,.

(103a)

It will prove useful to decompose the dipolar unnormalized enhancement functions as

\displaystyle\rho_{1}^{s}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=\rho_{1}^{s,00}(e_{t})+x\Big(\rho_{1}^{s,01}(e_{t})+\nu\rho_{1}^{s,11}(e_{t})\Big)

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{1}^{s}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=\tilde{\rho}_{1}^{s,00}(e_{t})+x\Big(\tilde{\rho}_{1}^{s,01}(e_{t})+\nu\tilde{\rho}_{1}^{s,11}(e_{t})\Big)

where

\displaystyle\rho_{1}^{s,00}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{5}\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\{-1,1\}\\
s\in\{-1,1\}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,00}_{i}\right)\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,00}_{i}\right)^{*}

(104a)

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{1}^{s,00}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{ijk}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{4}\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\{-1,1\}\\
s\in\{-1,1\}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,00}_{j}\right)\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,00}_{k}\right)^{*}\,,

(104b)

\displaystyle\rho_{1}^{s,01}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{5}\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\{-1,1\}\\
s\in\{-1,1\}\end{subarray}}\Bigg[\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,00}_{i}\right)\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,01}_{i}\right)^{*}+\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,01}_{i}\right)\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,00}_{i}\right)^{*}\Bigg]\,,

(104c)

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{1}^{s,01}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{ijk}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{4}\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\{-1,1\}\\
s\in\{-1,1\}\end{subarray}}\Bigg[\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,00}_{j}\right)\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,01}_{k}\right)^{*}+\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,01}_{j}\right)\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,00}_{k}\right)^{*}\Bigg]\,,

(104d)

\displaystyle\rho_{1}^{s,11}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{5}\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\{-1,1\}\\
s\in\{-1,1\}\end{subarray}}\Bigg[\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,00}_{i}\right)\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,11}_{i}\right)^{*}+\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,11}_{i}\right)\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,00}_{i}\right)^{*}\Bigg]\,,

(104e)

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{1}^{s,11}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{ijk}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{4}\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\{-1,1\}\\
s\in\{-1,1\}\end{subarray}}\Bigg[\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,00}_{j}\right)\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,11}_{k}\right)^{*}+\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,11}_{j}\right)\left({}^{m}_{\,\,p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}^{s,00}_{k}\right)^{*}\Bigg]

(104f)

The limits of these unnormalized enhancement functions when
e_{t}\rightarrow 0
are straightforward to compute, and read:

\displaystyle\rho_{2}(0)

\displaystyle=32\,,

\displaystyle\rho_{2}^{s}(0)

\displaystyle=32\,,

\displaystyle\rho_{1}^{s,00}(0)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\rho_{1}^{s,01}(0)

\displaystyle=-\frac{9}{5}\mathcal{A}\,,

\displaystyle\rho_{1}^{s,11}(0)

\displaystyle=\frac{14}{15}\,,

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}^{s}(0)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,,

\displaystyle\rho_{0}^{s}(0)

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{2}(0)

\displaystyle=16\mathrm{i}\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{2}^{s}(0)

\displaystyle=16\mathrm{i}\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{1}^{s,00}(0)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{1}^{s,01}(0)

\displaystyle=-\frac{9\mathrm{i}}{5}\mathcal{A}\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{1}^{s,11}(0)

\displaystyle=\frac{14\mathrm{i}}{15}\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\sigma}_{1}^{s}(0)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\,,

(105)

where I have introduced the recurring parameter

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle\equiv 1+\frac{5}{27}\bar{\gamma}+\frac{10}{27}\bar{\beta}_{+}-\frac{10}{9}\frac{\bar{\beta}_{+}}{\bar{\gamma}}-\frac{10}{9}\frac{\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}}{\bar{\gamma}\mathcal{S}_{-}}+\delta\left[-\frac{10}{27}\bar{\beta}_{-}+\frac{10}{9}\frac{\bar{\beta}_{-}}{\bar{\gamma}}-\frac{4}{9}\frac{\mathcal{S}_{+}}{\mathcal{S}_{-}}+\frac{10}{9}\frac{\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{+}}{\bar{\gamma}\mathcal{S}_{-}}\right]\,.

(106)

It will prove useful to note that
\rho_{0}^{s}(e_{t})\sim e_{t}/4
as
e_{t}\rightarrow 0
. I then express Eqs. (
102
) only in terms of the pair of variable
x
and
e_{t}
. For this, I use the 1PN QK parametrization
Trestini 2024
to expand the ADM mass, the mean motion and the periastron advance:

\displaystyle\mathrm{M}

\displaystyle=m\left(1-\frac{x\nu}{2}+\mathcal{O}(x^{2})\right)\,,

(107a)

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle=\frac{c^{3}x^{3/2}}{\tilde{G}\alpha m}\left(1-\frac{x\mathcal{B}}{1-e_{t}^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(x^{2})\right)\,,

(107b)

\displaystyle k

\displaystyle=\frac{x\mathcal{B}}{1-e_{t}^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(x^{2})\,,

(107c)

and where the reader is reminded that
\mathcal{B}
was defined in Eq. (
18
).

The expression for the fluxes thus obtained can finally be written similarly to the fluxes for circular orbits, in which some extra
normalized
enhancement functions of the eccentricity have been inserted. In the tensor sector, they are given by

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{tail}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{32c^{5}x^{5}\nu^{2}(1+\bar{\gamma}/2)}{5\tilde{G}\alpha}\times 4\pi(1+\bar{\gamma}/2)x^{3/2}\varphi_{2}(e_{t})+\mathcal{O}(x^{7})\,,

(108a)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{tail}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{32c^{2}x^{7/2}m\nu^{2}(1+\bar{\gamma}/2)}{5}\times 4\pi(1+\bar{\gamma}/2)x^{3/2}\tilde{\varphi}_{2}(e_{t})+\mathcal{O}(x^{7})\,,

(108b)

where the normalized enhancement functions read

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}(e_{t})=\frac{1}{32}\rho_{2}(e_{t})\qquad\qquad\text{and}\qquad\qquad\qquad\tilde{\varphi}_{2}(e_{t})=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{16}\tilde{\rho}_{2}(e_{t})\,,

(109)

where the reader is reminded that the unnormalized enhancement functions are given by (
103
).

Similarly, the scalar sector is given by

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{F}^{s,\mathrm{tail}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{c^{5}x^{5}\nu^{2}\zeta}{3\tilde{G}\alpha}\times 4\pi(1+\bar{\gamma}/2)\sqrt{x}\Bigg\{2\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\varphi^{s}_{1}(e_{t})

\displaystyle+x\Bigg[\frac{8}{5}\varphi_{2}^{s}(e_{t})\bigg(4\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}-4\mathcal{S}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}\delta-\bar{\gamma}\zeta^{-1}-8\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\nu\bigg)+\frac{41}{15}\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\nu\theta^{s}_{1}(e_{t})

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{4}{15}\alpha^{s}_{1}(e_{t})\bigg(-27\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}-10\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}+30\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\bar{\gamma}^{-1}-5\bar{\gamma}\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}+30\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}\bar{\gamma}^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\delta\left[10\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}-30\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\bar{\gamma}^{-1}+12\mathcal{S}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}-30\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}\bar{\gamma}^{-1}\right]\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{3}e_{t}^{2}\varphi^{s}_{0}(e_{t})\bigg(48\bar{\beta}_{+}\zeta^{-1}-72\bar{\beta}_{+}^{2}\zeta^{-1}\bar{\gamma}^{-1}-8\bar{\gamma}\zeta^{-1}+17\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}+144\bar{\beta}_{-}^{2}\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\bar{\gamma}^{-2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+144\bar{\beta}_{+}^{2}\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\bar{\gamma}^{-2}-96\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\bar{\gamma}^{-1}+288\bar{\beta}_{-}\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}\bar{\gamma}^{-2}-96\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}\bar{\gamma}^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\delta\left[24\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\bar{\gamma}^{-1}-8\mathcal{S}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}+24\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}\bar{\gamma}^{-1}\right]-4\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\nu\bigg)\Bigg]\Bigg\}+\mathcal{O}(x^{7})\,,

(110a)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{G}^{s,\mathrm{tail}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{c^{2}x^{7/2}m\nu^{2}\zeta}{3}\times 4\pi(1+\bar{\gamma}/2)\sqrt{x}\Bigg\{2\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\tilde{\varphi}^{s}_{1}(e_{t})

\displaystyle+x\Bigg[\frac{8}{5}\tilde{\varphi}_{2}^{s}(e_{t})\bigg(4\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}-4\mathcal{S}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}\delta-\bar{\gamma}\zeta^{-1}-8\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\nu\bigg)+\frac{41}{15}\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\nu\tilde{\theta}^{s}_{1}(e_{t})

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{4}{15}\tilde{\alpha}^{s}_{1}(e_{t})\bigg(-27\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}-10\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}+30\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\bar{\gamma}^{-1}-5\bar{\gamma}\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}+30\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}\gamma^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\delta\left[10\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}-30\bar{\beta}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\bar{\gamma}^{-1}+12\mathcal{S}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}-30\bar{\beta}_{+}\mathcal{S}_{-}\mathcal{S}_{+}\bar{\gamma}^{-1}\right]\bigg)\Bigg]\Bigg\}+\mathcal{O}(x^{7})\,,

(110b)

where the enhancement functions read

\displaystyle\varphi^{s}_{0}(e_{t})=\frac{4\,\rho^{s}_{0}(e_{t})}{e_{t}^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\varphi^{s}_{2}(e_{t})=\frac{1}{32}\,\rho^{s}_{2}(e_{t})\,,\quad

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}^{s}_{2}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{16}\tilde{\rho}^{s}_{2}(e_{t})\,,

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}^{s}(e_{t})=2\,\rho_{1}^{s,00}(e_{t})\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}^{s}_{1}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=-2\mathrm{i}\tilde{\rho}_{1}^{s,00}(e_{t})\,,

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}^{s}(e_{t})=-\frac{5}{9\mathcal{A}}\Big[\rho_{1}^{s,01}(e_{t})+\frac{5\mathcal{B}}{1-e_{t}^{2}}\left(\sigma_{1}^{s}(e_{t})-\rho_{1}^{s,00}(e_{t})\right)\Big]\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}^{s}_{1}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{5\mathrm{i}}{9\mathcal{A}}\Big[\tilde{\rho}_{1}^{s,01}(e_{t})+\frac{4\mathcal{B}}{1-e_{t}^{2}}\left(\tilde{\sigma}_{1}^{s}(e_{t})-\tilde{\rho}_{1}^{s,00}(e_{t})\right)\Big]\,,

\displaystyle\theta^{s}_{1}(e_{t})=-\frac{30}{41}\left(\rho_{1}^{s,00}(e_{t})-2\rho_{1}^{s,11}(e_{t})\right)\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}^{s}_{1}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{30\mathrm{i}}{41}\left(\tilde{\rho}_{1}^{s,00}(e_{t})-2\tilde{\rho}_{1}^{s,11}(e_{t})\right)\,,

(111)

and where the reader is reminded that the unnormalized enhancement functions are given by (
103
).
The normalized enhancement functions are defined such that they tend to
1
when
e_{t}\rightarrow 0
. I chose to normalize it such that
\varphi_{0}^{s}(e_{t})\sim e_{t}^{2}
when
e_{t}\rightarrow 0
).
Thus, taking the
e_{t}\rightarrow 0
limit and setting all enhancement functions to
1
in (
108
) and (
110
)
immediately yields the expressions for the tail sector of the circular energy flux. For the energy fluxes, it can be verified that the circular limit for the tail sector agrees with the odd PN-order terms in (5.3) of
35
. Moreover, it is immediate to verify that in the circular limit,
\big\langle\mathcal{F}_{\text{circ}}^{\mathrm{tail}}\big\rangle=\omega\,\big\langle\mathcal{G}_{\text{circ}}^{\mathrm{tail}}\big\rangle
and
\big\langle\mathcal{F}_{\text{circ}}^{s,\mathrm{tail}}\,\big\rangle=\omega\,\big\langle\mathcal{G}_{\text{circ}}^{s,\mathrm{tail}}\big\rangle
.

Of particular interest will be the small-eccentricity expansions of these enhancement functions:

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}(e_{t})=

\displaystyle=1+\frac{2335}{192}e_{t}^{2}+\frac{42955}{768}e_{t}^{4}+\frac{6204647}{36864}e_{t}^{6}+\frac{352891481}{884736}e_{t}^{8}+\frac{286907786543}{353894400}e_{t}^{10}+\mathcal{O}(e_{t}^{12})\,,

(112a)

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}_{2}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=1+\frac{209}{32}e_{t}^{2}+\frac{2415}{128}e_{t}^{4}+\frac{730751}{18432}e_{t}^{6}+\frac{10355719}{147456}e_{t}^{8}+\frac{6594861233}{58982400}e_{t}^{10}+\mathcal{O}(e_{t}^{12})\,,

(112b)

\displaystyle\varphi^{s}_{2}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=1+\frac{2335}{192}e_{t}^{2}+\frac{42955}{768}e_{t}^{4}+\frac{6204647}{36864}e_{t}^{6}+\frac{352891481}{884736}e_{t}^{8}+\frac{286907786543}{353894400}e_{t}^{10}+\mathcal{O}(e_{t}^{12})\,,

(112c)

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}^{s}_{2}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=1+\frac{209}{32}e_{t}^{2}+\frac{2415}{128}e_{t}^{4}+\frac{730751}{18432}e_{t}^{6}+\frac{10355719}{147456}e_{t}^{8}+\frac{6594861233}{58982400}e_{t}^{10}+\mathcal{O}(e_{t}^{12})\,,

(112d)

\displaystyle\varphi^{s}_{1}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=1+7e_{t}^{2}+\frac{717}{32}e_{t}^{4}+\frac{7435}{144}e_{t}^{6}+\frac{7305575}{73728}e_{t}^{8}+\frac{103947697}{614400}e_{t}^{10}+\mathcal{O}(e_{t}^{12})\,,

(112e)

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}^{s}_{1}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=1+3e_{t}^{2}+\frac{179}{32}e_{t}^{4}+\frac{1249}{144}e_{t}^{6}+\frac{99715}{8192}e_{t}^{8}+\frac{29573111}{1843200}e_{t}^{10}+\mathcal{O}(e_{t}^{12})\,,

(112f)

\displaystyle\alpha^{s}_{1}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=1+\frac{1}{\mathcal{A}^{2}}\sum_{n=2}^{10}\mathcal{C}_{n}e_{t}^{n}+\mathcal{O}(e_{t}^{2})\,,

(112g)

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}^{s}_{1}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=1+\frac{1}{\mathcal{A}^{2}}\sum_{n=2}^{10}\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{n}e_{t}^{n}+\mathcal{O}(e_{t}^{2})\,,

(112h)

\displaystyle\theta^{s}_{1}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=1+\frac{317}{41}e_{t}^{2}+\frac{36837}{1312}e_{t}^{4}+\frac{214765}{2952}e_{t}^{6}+\frac{469593535}{3022848}e_{t}^{8}+\frac{7363736527}{25190400}e_{t}^{10}+\mathcal{O}(e_{t}^{12})\,,

(112i)

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}^{s}_{1}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=1+\frac{153}{41}e_{t}^{2}+\frac{11179}{1312}e_{t}^{4}+\frac{46327}{2952}e_{t}^{6}+\frac{25733108}{1007616}e_{t}^{8}+\frac{2895685801}{75571200}e_{t}^{10}+\mathcal{O}(e_{t}^{12})\,,

(112j)

\displaystyle\varphi^{s}_{0}(e_{t})

\displaystyle=1+\frac{31}{4}e_{t}^{2}+\frac{2771}{96}e_{t}^{4}+\frac{177151}{2304}e_{t}^{6}+\frac{62037619}{368640}e_{t}^{8}+\mathcal{O}(e_{t}^{10})\,,

(112k)

where
\mathcal{A}
is given in (
106
), and the coefficients
\mathcal{C}_{n}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
are given explicitly in App.
C
[they depend in a complicated manner on the scalar-tensor parameters and are therefore very lengthy].

Although these expansions are formally only valid for small eccentricities, they can be resummed by factoring out
(1-e_{t}^{2})^{-N/2}
, where
N
is an integer to be determined for each enhancement function. This determination can either be done heuristically, by comparison to the numerically-evaluated function, or using asymptotic analysis in the lines of IV.C. of Ref.
Forseth et al. 2016
(see also
Loutrel and Yunes 2017
for an alternative asymptotic analysis). Despite their simplicity, these resummations can dramatically improve the accuracy of the approximation for high eccentricities.

VI.5
The memory flux

Finally, I tackle the case of the memory integral, given by (
86
). Such an integral appears in GR for the first time at 2.5PN, and was first studied in Ref.
Arun et al. 2009
. The latter work found that the magnitude of the memory effect was infinite in the limit where the initial eccentricity of the system is
1
, i.e., in the limit where the binary was formed in the infinite past [see their Eq. (A11)]. To mitigate this problem, the authors then invoke the astrophysical argument that in practice, the binary must have formed by capture with some initial eccentricity. Still, this suggests that the waveform is dominated by the remote past, and the zero-eccentricity limit is not smooth. That method was recently adapted to ST theories in Ref.
Jain and Rettegno 2024
, whose Eq. (4.19) also diverges
8
8

8

In (4.19) of
Jain and Rettegno 2024
, replace
a
by its expression in terms of
e
given by (
76b
), and perform the change of variables
t\rightarrow e
using (
76a
). The integral then reads (for
\kappa\equiv{(\tilde{G}\alpha m)^{2}\zeta\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\nu}/{c^{3}}
):

\displaystyle\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{0}}\differential t\frac{e^{2}(e^{2}+6)}{a^{4}(e^{2}-1)^{4}}=-\frac{1}{2\kappa c_{0}}\int_{e_{1}}^{e_{0}}\differential e\frac{6+e^{2}}{(1-e^{2})^{3/2}e^{1/3}}=-\frac{1}{2\kappa c_{0}}\left[e^{2/3}\left\{\frac{7}{\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}+2F\left(\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{2};\frac{4}{3};e^{2}\right)\right\}\right]^{e_{0}}_{e_{1}}\sim\frac{7}{2c_{0}\kappa}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-e_{1}^{2}}}\quad\text{as}\ e_{1}\rightarrow 1\,.

as the initial eccentricity goes to
1
.

When reexamining this problem, I found that these aforementioned divergences are cured by a careful split between AC and DC pieces. I will now describe in detail the full treatment of the memory integral in the case of ST theory, and the complete treatment of the GR case is relegated to Appendix
A
.

For a nonprecessing orbit, the direction of the angular momentum flux is constant and points in the
z
-direction. It is then straightforward to explicitly express its norm
\mathcal{G}=|\vec{\mathcal{G}}|=\mathcal{G}_{z}
in terms of the
(x,y,z)
components, namely

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{mem}}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{G^{2}\phi_{0}(3+2\omega_{0})}{15c^{8}}\Bigg\{\left(\mathrm{I}_{xx}^{(3)}(u)-\mathrm{I}_{yy}^{(3)}(u)\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,f(u-\tau)-\mathrm{I}_{xy}^{(3)}(u)\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,g(u-\tau)\Bigg\}\,,

(113)

where I have used the fact that
\mathrm{I}_{xz}=\mathrm{I}_{yz}=0
and defined

f=\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}_{x}^{s}\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}_{y}^{s}\qquad\qquad\text{and}\qquad\qquad g=\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}_{x}^{s}\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}_{x}^{s}-\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}_{y}^{s}\overset{(2)}{\mathrm{I}}{\!}_{y}^{s}\,.

(114)

In order to distinguish the AC and DC contributions, I decompose these functions into orbit-averaged and zero-average contributions, namely
f=\left\langle f\right\rangle\!+\tilde{f}
and
g=\left\langle g\right\rangle\!+\tilde{g}
. Consequently, the functions
\tilde{f}
and
\tilde{g}
contain purely oscillatory terms which average out to zero over a single orbit. Thus, it is now possible to define precisely the AC and DC contributions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{DC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{G^{2}\phi_{0}(3+2\omega_{0})}{15c^{8}}\Bigg\{\left(\mathrm{I}_{xx}^{(3)}(u)-\mathrm{I}_{yy}^{(3)}(u)\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,\left\langle f\right\rangle\!(u-\tau)\ -\mathrm{I}_{xy}^{(3)}(u)\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,\left\langle g\right\rangle\!(u-\tau)\Bigg\}\,,

(115a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{AC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{G^{2}\phi_{0}(3+2\omega_{0})}{15c^{8}}\Bigg\{\left(\mathrm{I}_{xx}^{(3)}(u)-\mathrm{I}_{yy}^{(3)}(u)\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,\tilde{f}(u-\tau)-\mathrm{I}_{xy}^{(3)}(u)\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,\tilde{g}(u-\tau)\Bigg\}\,.

(115b)

VI.5.1
The AC part

First, I will focus on the AC terms. Replacing the components of the quadrupole moments by their Fourier series following the notations of Sec.
IV
, I find that

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=n^{4}\sum_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q)\ell}\,p^{2}q^{2}\ {}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}\ {}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{y}^{s}\,,

(116a)

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=n^{4}\sum_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q)\ell}\,p^{2}q^{2}\left({}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}\ {}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}-{}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{y}^{s}\ {}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{y}^{s}\right)\,.

(116b)

By definition of the orbital average, the DC component exactly corresponds to the terms for which
p+q=0
, so I can now simply reexpress the AC term as

\displaystyle\tilde{f}

\displaystyle=n^{4}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q)\ell}\,p^{2}q^{2}\ {}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}\ {}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}\,,

(117a)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}

\displaystyle=n^{4}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q)\ell}\,p^{2}q^{2}\left({}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}\ {}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}-{}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{y}^{s}\ {}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{y}^{s}\right)\,.

(117b)

Replacing all moments by their expressions as Fourier series (
23
), replacing the normalizations of the Fourier coefficients by their expressions (
28
), performing the time integrals explicitly, and substituting at leading order
n=c^{3}x^{3/2}/(\tilde{G}\alpha m)
, I readily obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{AC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4}{15}\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\zeta(1+\bar{\gamma}/2)m\nu^{3}c^{2}x^{5}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}e^{\mathrm{i}(r+p+q)\ell}\,\frac{p^{2}q^{2}r^{3}}{p+q}\,\mathcal{S}_{r,p,q}\,,

(118)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{r,p,q}

\displaystyle=\Big({}_{r}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{xx}-{}_{r}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{yy}\Big){}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}\ {}_{q}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{y}^{s}-{}_{r}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{xy}\Big({}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}\ {}_{q}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{x}^{s}-{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{y}^{s}\ {}_{q}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{y}^{s}\Big)\,.

(119)

Here, I expressed
\mathcal{S}_{r,p,q}
in terms of the normalized Fourier coefficients, given by (
29
). Thanks to the latter expressions, it is immediate to see that
\mathcal{S}_{r,p,q}\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
. Since
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{AC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}\in\mathbb{R}
by construction, taking the real part of (
118
) yields the explicitly real-valued expression:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{AC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4}{15}\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\zeta(1+\bar{\gamma}/2)m\nu^{3}c^{2}x^{5}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}\sin\Big((r+p+q)\ell\Big)\,\frac{p^{2}q^{2}r^{3}}{p+q}\,\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}_{r,p,q}\,.

(120)

In order to compute the orbit-average of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{AC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}
, it is in fact more practical to start from the expression (
118
), and to find

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{AC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{15}\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\zeta(1+\bar{\gamma}/2)m\nu^{3}c^{2}x^{5}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}p^{2}q^{2}(p+q)^{2}\,\mathcal{S}_{-p-q,p,q}\,.

(121)

Since
\mathcal{S}_{-p-q,p,q}
is purely imaginary, it follows that
\left\langle\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{AC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}\right\rangle\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
, but of course it is also true by construction that
\left\langle\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{AC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}\right\rangle\in\mathbb{R}
. All this implies that, necessarily,

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{AC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}\right\rangle=0\,.

(122)

VI.5.2
The DC part

I now turn to the DC part. Thanks to the explicit expression of the quadrupole moment and upon careful computation of the orbital average, I obtain
\langle f\rangle=0
and

\displaystyle\langle g\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{G}^{2}\alpha^{3}\zeta^{2}\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}m^{4}\nu^{2}}{(1-\zeta)^{2}\phi_{0}^{2}}\times\frac{e^{2}}{a^{4}(1-e^{2})^{5/2}}\,.

(123)

Thanks to the Eqs. (
76
), I can reexpress the nonvanishing memory integral as an integral over eccentricity. In the far past, the eccentricity asymptotes towards one, so the memory integral reads

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\differential\tau\ \langle g\rangle(u-\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{c^{3}\alpha\zeta m^{2}\nu}{2(1-\zeta)^{2}\phi_{0}^{2}c_{0}}\int_{e}^{1}\frac{\differential\tilde{e}}{\tilde{e}^{1/3}}=\frac{3c^{3}\alpha\zeta m^{2}\nu}{4(1-\zeta)^{2}\phi_{0}^{2}c_{0}}(1-e^{2/3})\,.

(124)

Finally, plugging (
124
) into (
115a
) leads to

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{DC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(\tilde{G}\alpha m)^{2}\nu\phi_{0}(1+\bar{\gamma}/2)}{20c^{5}}\frac{e^{4/3}}{a(1+e^{2/3}+e^{4/3})}\,\mathrm{I}_{xy}^{(3)}\,,

(125)

where
c_{0}
has been replaced by its expression (
76b
) in terms of
a
and
e
. Thus, one can now see that
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{DC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}\rightarrow 0
in the circularized limit
e\rightarrow 0
. Remarkably, this conclusion is perfectly consistent with the result one would have obtained if one had instead computed the DC memory integral using an eternally quasicircular model
Blanchet et al. 2002
.

Finally, since the orbital parameters are (approximately) constant over one orbit, and since
\langle\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}\rangle=0
, I finally find that

\left\langle\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{DC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}\right\rangle=0\,.

(126)

VI.6
Recapitulation

Having reviewed the different contributions to the orbit-averaged energy and angular momentum flux, let me summarize the results:

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{F}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{inst}}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{tail}}\right\rangle\,,

(127a)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{G}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{inst}}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{tail}}\right\rangle\,,

(127b)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{F}^{s}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathcal{F}^{s,\mathrm{inst}}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathcal{F}^{s,\mathrm{tail}}\right\rangle\,,

(127c)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{G}^{s}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathcal{G}^{s,\mathrm{inst}}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathcal{G}^{s,\mathrm{tail}}\right\rangle\,.

(127d)

The
tensorial instantaneous
fluxes
\left\langle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{inst}}\right\rangle
and
\left\langle\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{inst}}\right\rangle
are given by (
92
), whereas the
scalar instantaneous
fluxes
\left\langle\mathcal{F}^{s,\mathrm{inst}}\right\rangle
and
\left\langle\mathcal{G}^{s,\mathrm{inst}}\right\rangle
are given (
93
).
The
tensorial tail
fluxes
\left\langle\mathcal{F}^{s,\mathrm{tail}}\right\rangle
and
\left\langle\mathcal{G}^{s,\mathrm{tail}}\right\rangle
are instead given by (
108
), whereas the
scalar tail
fluxes
\left\langle\mathcal{F}^{s,\mathrm{tail}}\right\rangle
and
\left\langle\mathcal{G}^{s,\mathrm{tail}}\right\rangle
are given by (
110
). Note that the tail fluxes are given in terms of some
enhancement
functions of the eccentricity, defined exactly in (
109
) and (
VI.4
), and as a small eccentricity expansion in (
112
).
All other nonlocal terms (namely,
\mathcal{G}_{i}^{s,\bm{\Pi}^{s}}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\text{mem}}
) vanish upon orbit averaging. Note that
after orbit averaging
and at this order, there is a clean separation between the instantaneous terms, which enter at integer PN order, and the hereditary tail terms, which appear at half-integer PN orders. This is analogous to the observation made in general relativity
Arun et al. 2008b
, and one can expect this split to be broken at the level of the tails-of-tails.

VII
Evolution of the orbital elements

Thanks to both the QK parametrization of Section
III
and the fluxes obtained in Section
VI
, it is now possible to obtain from flux-balance arguments the secular evolution of the QK parameters under the effect of radiation reaction at 1.5PN order, i.e. at relative 2.5PN beyond the leading radiation reaction.

Consider an orbital element, which is denoted generically
\xi
. Its orbit-averaged evolution can be expressed as

\left\langle\frac{\differential\xi}{\differential t}\right\rangle=\frac{\partial\xi}{\partial E}\left\langle\frac{\differential E}{\differential t}\right\rangle+\frac{\partial\xi}{\partial J}\left\langle\frac{\differential J}{\differential t}\right\rangle\,.

(128)

where
J=|\mathbf{J}|
.
Since the expressions of the orbital elements in terms of
E
and
J
were computed in Sec.
III
and the energy and angular momentum fluxes were computed in Sec.
VI
, we now have all the ingredients to compute the expressions of the orbital elements, assuming that the following balance equations hold:

\left\langle\frac{\differential E}{\differential t}\right\rangle=-\left\langle\mathcal{F}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathcal{F}^{s}\right\rangle\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\left\langle\frac{\differential J}{\differential t}\right\rangle=-\left\langle\mathcal{G}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathcal{G}^{s}\right\rangle\,.

(129)

Only the evolution equations for the pair of orbital elements
(x,e_{t})
is presented — once these are solved (numerically), the other orbital elements can be straightforwardly deduced through the QK parametrization of
47
. Note that the 2PN-accurate QK parametrization must also be used to express
(E,J)
in terms of
(x,e_{t})
. The evolution equations read

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{\differential x}{\differential t}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{2c^{3}\zeta\nu x^{4}}{3\tilde{G}\alpha m}\Bigg\{\frac{4\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\left(1+\tfrac{1}{2}e_{t}^{2}\right)}{(1-e_{t}^{2})^{5/2}}+\frac{x}{15(1-e_{t}^{2})^{7/2}}\Big(\mathfrak{X}_{1}+e_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{X}_{2}+e_{t}^{4}\mathfrak{X}_{3}\Big)+8\pi\left(1+\tfrac{1}{2}\bar{\gamma}\right)\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\varphi^{s}_{1}(e_{t})\,x^{3/2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+x^{2}\Bigg(\frac{\mathfrak{X}_{4}+e_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{X}_{5}+e_{t}^{4}\mathfrak{X}_{6}+e_{t}^{6}\mathfrak{X}_{7}}{(1-e_{t}^{2})^{9/2}}+\frac{\mathfrak{X}_{8}+e_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{X}_{9}+e_{t}^{4}\mathfrak{X}_{10}}{(1-e_{t}^{2})^{4}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+4\pi\left(1+\tfrac{1}{2}\bar{\gamma}\right)x^{5/2}\Bigg(\mathcal{X}_{11}\varphi_{2}(e_{t})+\mathcal{X}_{12}\varphi^{s}_{2}(e_{t})+\mathcal{X}_{13}\alpha^{s}_{1}(e_{t})+\mathcal{X}_{14}\theta^{s}_{1}(e_{t})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\Big(\mathcal{X}_{15}+e_{t}^{2}\mathcal{X}_{16}\Big)\frac{\varphi^{s}_{1}(e_{t})}{1-e_{t}^{2}}+\mathcal{X}_{17}\frac{\tilde{\varphi}^{s}_{1}(e_{t})}{(1-e_{t}^{2})^{3/2}}+\mathcal{X}_{18}e_{t}^{2}\varphi^{s}_{0}(e_{t})\Bigg)+\mathcal{O}(x^{3})\Bigg\}\,,

(130a)

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{\differential e_{t}}{\differential t}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{c^{3}\zeta\nu x^{3}e_{t}}{\tilde{G}\alpha m}\Bigg\{\frac{2\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}}{(1-e_{t}^{2})^{3/2}}+\frac{x}{15(1-e_{t}^{2})^{5/2}}\Big(\mathfrak{E}_{1}+e_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{E}_{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{8\pi}{3}\left(1+\tfrac{1}{2}\bar{\gamma}\right)\mathcal{S}_{-}^{2}\frac{1-e_{t}^{2}}{e_{t}^{2}}\,\Big(\varphi^{s}_{1}(e_{t})-\frac{\tilde{\varphi}^{s}_{1}}{\sqrt{1-e_{t}^{2}}}\Big)x^{3/2}+x^{2}\Bigg(\frac{\mathfrak{E}_{3}+e_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{E}_{4}+e_{t}^{4}\mathfrak{E}_{5}}{(1-e_{t}^{2})^{7/2}}+\frac{\mathfrak{E}_{6}+e_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{E}_{7}}{(1-e_{t}^{2})^{3}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+4\pi\left(1+\tfrac{1}{2}\bar{\gamma}\right)x^{5/2}\Bigg[\mathfrak{E}_{8}\frac{1-e_{t}^{2}}{e_{t}^{2}}\Big(\varphi_{2}(e_{t})-\frac{\tilde{\varphi}_{2}}{\sqrt{1-e_{t}^{2}}}\Big)+\mathfrak{E}_{9}\frac{1-e_{t}^{2}}{e_{t}^{2}}\Big(\varphi^{s}_{2}(e_{t})-\frac{\tilde{\varphi}^{s}_{2}}{\sqrt{1-e_{t}^{2}}}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\mathfrak{E}_{10}\frac{1-e_{t}^{2}}{e_{t}^{2}}\Big(\alpha^{s}_{1}(e_{t})-\frac{\tilde{\alpha}^{s}_{2}}{\sqrt{1-e_{t}^{2}}}\Big)+\mathfrak{E}_{11}\frac{1-e_{t}^{2}}{e_{t}^{2}}\Big(\theta^{s}_{1}(e_{t})-\frac{\tilde{\theta}^{s}_{1}}{\sqrt{1-e_{t}^{2}}}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{\mathfrak{E}_{12}}{e_{t}^{2}}\Big(\varphi^{s}_{1}(e_{t})-\frac{\tilde{\varphi}^{s}_{1}}{\sqrt{1-e_{t}^{2}}}\Big)+\mathfrak{E}_{13}\varphi_{1}^{s}(e_{t})+\frac{\mathfrak{E}_{14}}{\sqrt{1-e_{t}^{2}}}\tilde{\varphi}_{1}^{s}(e_{t})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\mathfrak{E}_{15}(1-e_{t}^{2})\varphi_{0}^{s}(e_{t})\Bigg]+\mathcal{O}(x^{3})\Bigg\}\,,

(130b)

where the expressions of the coefficients
\mathfrak{X}_{n}
and
\mathfrak{E}_{n}
are relegated to App.
E
.

In Eq. (
130
), at first sight, there seems to be some divergent terms when
e_{t}\rightarrow 0
for the half-integer PN orders. However, this is fortunately not the case, because the enhancement functions, denoted here generically by
\varphi(e_{t})
and
\tilde{\varphi}(e_{t})
, appear as the combination
9
9

9

The global prefactor
e_{t}
has been included.

e_{t}^{-1}[\varphi(e_{t})-(1-e_{t}^{2})^{-1/2}\tilde{\varphi}(e_{t})]
, which behaves as
\mathcal{O}(e_{t})
in the
e_{t}\rightarrow 0
limit. This means not only that the limit for quasicircular orbits is well-defined, but also that eccentricity evolution is quenched when going to the quasicircular orbit limit, which means that quasicircular orbits are indeed stable.

VIII
Conclusion

In this work on ST theories, I obtained the orbit-averaged fluxes of energy and angular momentum for quasielliptic orbits at 1.5PN beyond quadrupolar radiation, i.e. relative 2.5PN beyond the leading
-1
PN dipolar radiation. This extends the results of
47
where these results were obtained at Newtonian order, as well as those of
35
, where these results were obtained in the limiting case of circular orbits at 1.5PN order. From these results, I was able to derive the 1.5PN secular evolution of the orbital elements
x
and
e_{t}
(the evolution of the other orbital elements are then straightforward to obtain thanks to the results of
47
). The secular evolution of the orbital parameters under radiation reaction is the eccentric-orbit analog of the frequency chirp in the case of circular orbits, as is hence the main observable in the GW signal. These results are part of a general program aiming at obtaining a full GW template bank for alternative theories of gravity. In the future, these results should be included into EOB models
Julié and Deruelle 2017
;
Julié 2018
;
Julié et al. 2022
;
Jain et al. 2023
;
Jain 2023
and hybridized with NR models
Ma et al. 2023
;
Corman and East 2024
in order to obtain full inspiral-merger-ringdown waveform templates. Although these theories are already heavily constrained by binary pulsar experiments, these waveform models could help constrain these theories even further, in particular with third-generation gravitational wave detectors. Moreover, they could be used in searches for GW signals which deviate strongly from the GR prediction, and could have been missed during GW searches, since the latter rely on matched filtering with pure GR waveforms.

Finally, I would like to stress that studying such alternative theories of gravity has a theoretical interest in its own right, and helps with our understanding of GR. This work was the occasion to revisit the classical GR derivation of the memory contribution to the angular momentum flux
Arun et al. 2009
. It was found that the spurious divergence in the initial eccentricity is cured by through a more careful split between DC and AC contributions. The simplicity of the Peters formula in ST tensor theory (
76
), with respect to its GR counterpart (
145
), means that using it to study hereditary effects provides a useful toy model to better understand GR phenomenology — compare the simple ST expression (
125
) with the GR expression (
150
), which is expressed in terms of hypergeometric functions. This is particularly true for the study of the hereditary terms arising from the passage to the center of mass, see Sec.
V
. These terms were recently discovered in GR
Blanchet et al. 2024
and evaluated for a quasicircular inspiral model. In this work, they appeared in the ST context, and were studied for the first time using an quasielliptic inspiral model. In the term associated to the translation between the matter content and the center of mass frame, I found that taking the vanishing eccentricity limit did not smoothly recover the quasicircular model, see Sec.
V.3
. This prompted me to revisit this problem in the GR scenario, for which the expressions are much more complicated and require evaluating integrals involving hypergeometric functions, and I found a completely analogous phenomenology
Trestini
, which will prompt further investigations. This work was also the occasion to introduce some technology which could prove useful in GR calculations in the future, such as the special function
\Theta^{p}_{q}(e_{t})
involved in the 1PN Fourier coefficients of the scalar dipole moment.

Future developments could include: computing the ‘post-adiabatic’ short period contributions to the orbital phasing, in the lines of
Damour et al. 2004a
; completing the full waveform amplitude modes consistently at 1.5PN order; extending these results to the quasihyperbolic case; etc. Another avenue is to extend these templates to more complicated theories, such as Einstein-scalar-Gauss-Bonnet.

Note

While this paper was in preparation, I became aware that a similar computation was being performed by Jain and Rettegno
Jain and Rettegno 2024
. Before the publication of
Jain and Rettegno 2024
, we compared our results for the generic expression of the
instantaneous
part of the angular momentum flux at (i) 1PN in the scalar sector [Eqs. (5.10-15) of
Jain and Rettegno 2024
and the 1PN truncation of Eq. (
90
) of this paper]; and (ii) 1.5PN in the tensor sector [
Eqs. (4.7-8) of
Jain and Rettegno 2024
and Eq. (
90
) of this paper], and found perfect agreement.

50
), and Luc Blanchet for discussions about the memory contribution and the nonlocal terms appearing the passage to the center-of-mass frame. The author also thanks the Institut d’Astrophysique de Paris for its hospitality. Most computations were performed using the xAct library
Martín-García et al. 2012
for
Mathematica

Wolfram Research, Inc. 2023
. The author acknowledges support from the Czech Academy of Sciences under the Grant No. LQ100102101 and from the ERC Consolidator/UKRI Frontier
Research Grant GWModels (selected by the ERC and funded by UKRI [grant number
EP/Y008251/1]).

Appendix A
Revisiting the memory contribution to the angular momentum flux in general relativity

The present work in scalar-tensor theory has been the occasion to revisit the problem of the angular momentum flux in general relativity, and correct some inconsistencies in the literature. Thus, in this section of the Appendix, I shall assume that the underlying theory of gravity is general relativity. The first study of the memory contribution to the angular momentum flux was done by Arun
et al.

Arun et al. 2009
. In that work, it is claimed that the AC contribution vanishes upon orbit averaging, but that the DC contribution is nonvanishing, even after orbit averaging. Strikingly, it is found in their Eq. (A12) that the orbit-averaged DC contribution depends strongly on some initial eccentricity
e_{1}
at which the binary was formed, and
diverges
as
\sim(1-e_{1}^{2})^{-1}
when
e_{1}\rightarrow 1
(see their Eq. (A11). Despite the argument that a compact binary cannot have existed eternally, their conclusions seem unphysical from the viewpoint of the formal two-body problem. Later, it was found by Loutrel and Yunes
Loutrel and Yunes 2017
that the orbit-averaged DC contribution should instead vanish upon orbit-averaging (see the end of their Sec. 6.2), using the argument that the DC contribution is proportional to
\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}
, which trivially vanishes after orbit averaging. However, they do not provide a satisfying explanation for the discrepancy (they invoke the choice of the initial phase, but this choice is arbitrary and should not affect the physical result). Moreover, when studying the DC contribution
before
orbit averaging, Loutrel and Yunes use a discrete model, valid for large eccentricities: the orbital parameters
(a,e)
are modeled as constant over an orbit, and updated discretely at every periastron passage, where most of the gravitational waves are emitted [see their Eq. (208)]. However, they do not address the convergence of their model when summing over the infinite past.

In the following, I will prove the following statements: (i) the AC and DC memory contributions vanish upon orbit averaging, and (ii) the DC contribution is nonvanishing but finite before orbit averaging. Moreover, the finite DC contribution vanishes in the limit of a circularized orbit (
e\rightarrow 0
), regardless of whether one uses a quasi-Keplerian or quasi-circular model for the past inspiral. The reason for the discrepancy is that Arun
et al.

Arun et al. 2009
decomposed their integrand as follows [see their Eq. (A4)]:

\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{mem}}=\sum_{n}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{0}}\differential tf_{n}(a,e)e^{\mathrm{i}n\phi}\,,

(131)

and identified the DC contribution with the
n=0
component [see their Eq. (A5)]. However, this implicitly assumes that the orbit-averaging procedure is
\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\differential\phi
, when instead it should be
10
10

10

I have used the expression of
\dot{\phi}
given by (A3c) of
Arun et al. 2009
, and used the fact that the orbital (radial) period reads
P=\frac{2\pi a^{3/2}}{\sqrt{Gm}}

\frac{1}{P}\int_{0}^{P}\differential t=\frac{1}{P}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\differential\phi}{\dot{\phi}}=\frac{(1-e^{2})^{3/2}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\differential\phi}{(1+e\cos\phi)^{2}}\,.

(132)

In the following, I revisit the computation of Arun
et al.

Arun et al. 2009
by carefully implementing of the orbit averaging procedure following (
132
).
At 3.5PN order, recall that the memory contribution to the angular momentum flux reads
Arun et al. 2009

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{i}^{\mathrm{mem}}=\frac{4G^{2}}{35c^{10}}\epsilon_{ijk}\mathrm{I}_{ja}^{(3)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,\left[\mathrm{I}_{kb}^{(3)}\mathrm{I}_{ab}^{(3)}\right](t_{\text{ret}}-\tau)\,.

(133)

For a nonprecessing orbit, the direction of the angular momentum flux is constant and points in the
z
-direction. It is then straightforward to explicitly express its norm
\mathcal{G}=|\vec{\mathcal{G}}|=\mathcal{G}_{z}
in terms of the
(x,y,z)
components:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{mem}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4G^{2}}{35c^{10}}\Bigg\{\left(\mathrm{I}_{xx}^{(3)}-\mathrm{I}_{yy}^{(3)}\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,f(t_{\text{ret}}-\tau)-\mathrm{I}_{xy}^{(3)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,g(t_{\text{ret}}-\tau)\Bigg\}\,,

(134)

where I used the fact that
\mathrm{I}_{xz}=\mathrm{I}_{yz}=0
and where I define

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=\mathrm{I}_{xy}^{(3)}\left(\mathrm{I}_{xx}^{(3)}+\mathrm{I}_{yy}^{(3)}\right)\,,

(135a)

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=\left(\mathrm{I}_{xx}^{(3)}\right)^{2}-\left(\mathrm{I}_{yy}^{(3)}\right)^{2}\,.

(135b)

In order to distinguish the AC and DC contributions, I decompose these functions into
f=\left\langle f\right\rangle\!+\tilde{f}
and
g=\left\langle g\right\rangle\!+\tilde{g}
, where
I have defined the orbit average as
\left\langle h\right\rangle=\frac{1}{P}\int_{0}^{P}\differential t\,h(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\differential\ell\,h(\ell)
. Here, I have introduced the mean anomaly
\ell=n(t-t_{0})=\frac{2\pi}{P}(t-t_{0})
, which will prove useful for the analysis. Consequently, the functions
\tilde{f}
and
\tilde{g}
contain purely oscillatory terms which average out to zero over a single orbit. Thus, I can now define precisely the AC and DC contributions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{DC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4G^{2}}{35c^{10}}\Bigg\{\left(\mathrm{I}_{xx}^{(3)}-\mathrm{I}_{yy}^{(3)}\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,\left\langle f\right\rangle\!(t_{\text{ret}}-\tau)-\mathrm{I}_{xy}^{(3)}(u)\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,\left\langle g\right\rangle\!(t_{\text{ret}}-\tau)\Bigg\}\,,

(136a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{AC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4G^{2}}{35c^{10}}\Bigg\{\left(\mathrm{I}_{xx}^{(3)}-\mathrm{I}_{yy}^{(3)}\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,\tilde{f}(t_{\text{ret}}-\tau)-\mathrm{I}_{xy}^{(3)}(u)\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,\tilde{g}(t_{\text{ret}}-\tau)\Bigg\}\,.

(136b)

A.1
The AC part

First, let me focus on the AC terms. I replace the components of the quadrupole moments by their Fourier series, which I denote as
\mathrm{I}_{ij}=\sum_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\ell}\ {}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}
. I find that

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=-n^{6}\sum_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q)\ell}\,p^{3}q^{3}\ {}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{xy}\left({}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{xx}+{}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{yy}\right)

(137a)

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=-n^{6}\sum_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q)\ell}\,p^{3}q^{3}\left({}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{xx}\ {}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{xx}-{}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{yy}\ {}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{yy}\right)

(137b)

By definition of the orbital average, the DC component is exactly defined by the terms for which
p+q=0
, so I can now simply reexpress the AC term as

\displaystyle\tilde{f}

\displaystyle=-n^{6}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q)\ell}\,p^{3}q^{3}\ {}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{xy}\left({}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{xx}+{}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{yy}\right)

(138a)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}

\displaystyle=-n^{6}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q)\ell}\,p^{3}q^{3}\left({}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{xx}\ {}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{xx}-{}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{yy}\ {}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{yy}\right)

(138b)

Using
\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}kn(t-t_{0}-\tau)}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}kn(t-t_{0})}/(ikn)
for
k\neq 0
, replacing all moments by their Fourier expansions, and substituting at leading order
n=c^{3}x^{3/2}/(Gm)
I readily obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{AC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}=\frac{4m\nu^{3}c^{2}x^{6}}{35}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}e^{\mathrm{i}(p+q+r)\ell}\,\frac{p^{3}q^{3}r^{3}}{p+q}\,\mathcal{S}_{r,p,q}\,,

(139)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{r,p,q}

\displaystyle=\left({}_{r}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{xx}-{}_{r}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{yy}\right){}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{xy}\left({}_{q}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{xx}+{}_{q}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{yy}\right)-{}_{r}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{xy}\left({}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{xx}\ {}_{q}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{xx}-{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{yy}\ {}_{q}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{yy}\right)\,.

(140)

Here, I expressed
\mathcal{S}_{r,p,q}
in terms of the normalized Fourier coefficients
{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}\equiv(m\nu a^{2})^{-1}{}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}
, which are explicitly given by (A3) of
Arun et al. 2008a
. Thanks to the latter expressions, it is immediate to see that
\mathcal{S}_{r,p,q}\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
. Since
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{AC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}\in\mathbb{R}
by construction, taking the real part of (
139
) yields the explicitly real-valued expression:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{AC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}=\frac{4m\nu^{3}c^{2}x^{6}}{35}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}\sin\Big((p+q+r)\ell\Big)\,\frac{p^{3}q^{3}r^{3}}{p+q}\,\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}_{r,p,q}\,,

(141)

In order to compute the orbit-average of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{AC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}
, it is in fact more practical to start directly from the expression (
139
), and to find

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{AC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}\right\rangle=-\frac{4m\nu^{3}c^{2}x^{6}}{35}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}p^{3}q^{3}(p+q)^{2}\,\mathcal{S}_{-p-q,p,q}\,.

(142)

Noting that on the one hand,
\left\langle\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{AC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}\right\rangle
is necessarily a real number, but on the other hand, it also purely imaginary (because
\mathcal{S}_{-p-q,p,q}\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
), I finally conclude that

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{AC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}\right\rangle=0\,.

(143)

A.2
The DC part

I now turn to the DC part. Thanks to the explicit expression of the quadrupole moment and upon careful computation of the orbital average, I obtain
\langle f\rangle=0
and

\displaystyle\langle g\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{G^{3}m^{5}\nu^{2}e^{2}(13+2e^{2})}{3a^{5}(1-e^{2})^{7/2}}\,.

(144)

Thanks to the standard relations derived by Peters
Peters 1964

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{\differential e}{\differential t}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{304G^{3}m^{3}\nu e}{15c^{5}a^{4}(1-e^{2})^{5/2}}\left(1+\frac{121}{304}e^{2}\right)\,,

(145a)

\displaystyle a(e)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{0}e^{12/19}}{1-e^{2}}\left(1+\frac{121}{304}e^{2}\right)^{\frac{870}{2299}}\,,

(145b)

I can reexpress the nonvanishing memory integral as an integral over eccentricity. In the far past, the eccentricity asymptotes towards one, so the memory integral reads

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\differential\tau\ \langle g\rangle(u-\tau)

\displaystyle=-\frac{5c^{5}m^{2}\nu}{304c_{0}}\int_{e}^{1}\differential\tilde{e}\,\frac{\tilde{e}^{7/19}(13+2\tilde{e}^{2})}{\left(1+\frac{121}{304}\tilde{e}^{2}\right)^{\frac{3169}{2299}}}=-\frac{5c^{5}m^{2}\nu}{304c_{0}}\mathcal{K}\,\mathcal{M}(e)\,,

(146)

where I have introduced the constant

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\differential\tilde{e}\,\frac{\tilde{e}^{7/19}(13+2\tilde{e}^{2})}{\left(1+\frac{121}{304}\tilde{e}^{2}\right)^{\frac{3169}{2299}}}=\frac{19}{2}\,F\!\left(\frac{13}{19},\frac{3169}{2299};\frac{32}{19};-\frac{121}{304}\right)+\frac{19}{32}\,F\!\left(\frac{3169}{2299},\frac{32}{19};\frac{51}{19};-\frac{121}{304}\right)\,,

(147)

as well as the following function of eccentricity:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(e)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{\mathcal{K}}\int_{e}^{1}\differential\tilde{e}\,\frac{\tilde{e}^{7/19}(13+2\tilde{e}^{2})}{\left(1+\frac{121}{304}\tilde{e}^{2}\right)^{3169/2299}}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{e^{26/19}}{\mathcal{K}}\Bigg\{\frac{19}{2}\,F\!\left(\frac{13}{19},\frac{3169}{2299};\frac{32}{19};-\frac{121}{304}e^{2}\right)+\frac{19}{32}e^{2}\,F\!\left(\frac{3169}{2299},\frac{32}{19};\frac{51}{19};-\frac{121}{304}e^{2}\right)\Bigg\}\,.

(148)

In these expressions,
F(a,b;c;d)
is the ordinary (or Gaussian) hypergeometric function, and I have chosen to normalize
\mathcal{M}(e)
such that
\mathcal{M}(0)=1
and
\mathcal{M}(1)=0
(see Fig.
3
). The constant
\mathcal{K}
takes the numerical value
\mathcal{K}\approx 8.29969
, and it is possible to approximate
\mathcal{M}(e)
with the following expansion (which leads to absolute errors smaller than
0.0015
over the whole range of
e\in[0,1]
)

\mathcal{M}(e)\approx 1-e^{26/19}\Big(1.145-0.184\,e^{2}+0.0511\,e^{4}-0.0163\,e^{6}+0.00548\,e^{8}\Big)\,.

(149)

Plugging (
146
) into (
134
) leads to the final expression

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{DC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}

\displaystyle=\!\frac{G^{2}m^{2}\nu\mathcal{K}}{532c^{5}}\frac{e^{12/29}}{a(1-e^{2})}\!\left(\!1+\frac{121}{304}e^{2}\!\right)^{\frac{870}{2299}}\!\!\!\!\mathcal{M}(e)\,\mathrm{I}_{xy}^{(3)}\,,

(150)

where
c_{0}
has been replaced by its expression (
145b
) in terms of
a
and
e
. Thus, one can now see that
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{DC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}\rightarrow 0
in the circularized limit
e\rightarrow 0
. Remarkably, this conclusion is perfectly consistent with the result one would have obtained if one had instead computed the DC memory integral using an eternally quasicircular model, in which case the angular momentum flux is proportional to the energy flux and not does feature any memory-like nonlocal term.

Figure 3:
Plot of
\mathcal{M}(e)

Finally, since the orbital parameters are (approximately) constant over one orbit, and since
\langle\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}\rangle=0
, I find that

\left\langle\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{DC}}^{\mathrm{mem}}\right\rangle=0\,.

(151)

Since the memory contribution vanishes under orbit-averaging, it does not lead to any
secular
contributions to the evolution of the orbital parameters. However,
before orbit averaging
, the memory contribution is nonvanishing and finite, so it can lead to some
post-adiabatic, oscillatory
contributions to the evolution of the orbital parameters, along the lines of
Damour et al. 2004b
. I will not attempt to compute these contributions here, but let me highlight that the results obtained above prove that these contributions should be finite even for an eternally isolated binary system.
Finally, let me mention that a complete treatment of this memory integral in the case of
unbound
quasihyperbolic orbits can be found in Ref.
Bini and Geralico 2021
.

Appendix F
Expression of the scalar dipole at 2.5PN order

Recall that the scalar dipole is decomposed into an instantaneous and a hereditary contribution:

\mathrm{I}_{i}^{s}=\mathrm{I}_{i}^{s,\text{inst}}+\mathrm{I}_{i}^{s,\bm{\Gamma}}\,.

(159)

The hereditary contribution appears at 2.5PN order and is given by

\mathrm{I}_{i}^{s,\bm{\Gamma}}=\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}\zeta(\mathcal{S}_{+}+\mathcal{S}_{-}\delta)}{(1-\zeta)\phi_{0}}\Gamma_{i}^{s}\,,

(160)

while the instantaneous contribution is given with 2.5PN accuracy by

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}_{i}^{s,\text{inst}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha^{1/2}\zeta m\nu r}{(1-\zeta)\phi_{0}}\Biggl(2\mathcal{S}_{-}n^{i}

Appendix G
Expression of the instantaneous angular momentum fluxes in terms of the phase space variables

Recall Eq. (
90
):
